**SECTION 3 – PROJECT INFORMATION **(PLEASE PRINT OR TYPE) 
Applicant may submit answers to this section on a separate document so long as the proposal answers each question in the order presented and the responses are numbered accordingly 
**Project Type: Vegetated Barriers**
**1. Project Summary **
Briefly describe your project including committed partners, expected implementation period, project costs and how the project will mitigate air quality impacts. 
Supported by the City of Arvin, Hyphae Design Laboratory proposes a pilot vegetated barrier project along a section of Tejon Highway or S Comanche Drive in Arvin, identified by the AB 617 Community Steering Committee (CSC) in this RFP as areas of interest for air quality mitigation. This pilot aims to demonstrate the effectiveness of vegetated barriers as a proven strategy for improving environmental health, while conducting a research study to evaluate their impacts under varying environmental conditions. By generating robust data on air quality improvements and other co-benefits, this project will hopefully inform future projects in both Arvin and nearby Lamont in subsequent rounds of funding. Beyond mitigating air pollution, the barrier will reduce extreme heat, noise, pesticide drift, and road dust, while enhancing safety, shade, and pedestrian comfort. The project establishes a replicable, community-driven model for scalable green infrastructure in rural communities across the Central Valley.
Working in coordination with City Engineer Sam Lux and the AB 617 Community Steering Committee (CSC), as well as with local landowners, the County of Kern, and Caltrans where necessary, Hyphae hopes to integrate the vegetative barrier into ongoing sidewalk and gutter improvements, leveraging these infrastructure upgrades to maximize efficiency, cost-effectiveness, and environmental and public health benefits. 
Combining rigorous site analysis, simulation modeling, and precision engineering, Hyphae Design Laboratory is a national leader in designing vegetated barriers for air filtration. Drawing on over a decade of experience in vegetated barrier implementation, we employ a comprehensive Design-Build-Operate-Maintain (DBOM) approach tailored to each site. Our approach ensures precisely calibrated design and installation, as well as carefully observed maintenance protocols, delivering both immediate air quality improvements and long-term project sustainability. This project’s research component will include pre- and post-implementation air quality monitoring, enabling a comparative analysis of barrier performance and their capacity to mitigate dust, pollutants, and heat.
Our approach has been shaped through direct collaboration with Rich Baldauf and Max Zhang, authors of the EPA guidelines referenced in this RFP. We have successfully implemented similar projects in Richmond, West Oakland, Stockton, Los Angeles, Louisville, and Dallas, leveraging evidence-based innovation in green infrastructure. Last October, in partnership with Little Manila Rising, Hyphae was awarded $2M from AB617 to fund urban greening and vegetated barriers in Stockton. And as with all our projects, our ongoing NIH- and NSF-funded monitoring efforts continue to evaluate and refine our designs to optimize water conservation and air filtration outcomes. By partnering with local landscape contractors, we align environmental justice with job creation, ensuring project benefits are shared broadly. This project builds on our proven success across California while marking an important new phase of design and implementation in rural areas historically overlooked for their air quality and heat mitigation needs. 
The project spans three years of implementation and established maintenance, followed by seven years of long-term maintenance. Over the first three to six months, we will evaluate potential sites, develop high-level designs, and coordinate with City Engineer Sam Lux and the AB 617 Community Steering Committee (CSC) to align on project feasibility and objectives. Leveraging lessons from our Stockton project, may also work with Kern County and Caltrans if opportunities present themselves for incorporating vegetative barriers into adjacent areas, including right-of-ways and drainage ditches. In the second half of the first year, we will focus on detailed design refinement, contractor coordination, and the preparation of final cost estimates. 
Planting is scheduled to begin in January 2026 to align with optimal seasonal conditions, requiring approximately one month to complete. Our timeline incorporates flexibility to ensure alignment with preceding project phases and ideal environmental conditions. Throughout the process, we will deploy Hyphae’s NSF-funded AdaptOS platform to synthesize aerial imagery, satellite data, and other environmental metrics into hyperlocal maps of the hotspots. These maps, combined with fluid dynamics modeling, will optimize barrier placement, configuration, and design.
<comment_start id=kix.1mb74c6q1gns>Project costs are estimated at $493,151, covering comprehensive site analysis, design development, construction, planting, and long-term maintenance, as well as rigorous before-and-after effectiveness monitoring. Based on the iTree Report, the project will annually sequester \_\_\_637,319.4\_\_ pounds of CO2 while removing significant quantities of other pollutants:
  - Remove \_\_1,574.29\_\_\_ pounds of O3
  - Remove \_\_\_216.99\_\_ pounds of NO2
  - Remove \_\_2.75\_\_\_ pounds of SO2
  - Remove \_\_16.26\_\_\_ pounds of PM 2.5<comment_end id=kix.1mb74c6q1gns>
To ensure the long-term maintenance and stewardship of the barrier, we propose two potential approaches to be explored in collaboration with Valley Air and the Arvin/Lamont Steering Committee. The first involves contracting a landscape crew to implement a 10-year maintenance plan, covering tasks such as site prep and installation, as well as watering, mulching, and tree replacement. This approach has been budgeted for and would ensure consistent care throughout the maintenance period.
The second, more innovative alternative—currently under investigation—would involve establishing an escrow account to disburse funds annually for long-term maintenance. This model, inspired by financial mechanisms used in wetland mitigation projects, could provide a sustainable framework for addressing the challenge of funding long-term care for environmental projects. While not implemented in this proposal, it represents an ideal model that could be scaled across District projects with the support of stakeholders. We are eager to continue a conversation with the Air District and CARB to explore how adopting a CARB stewardship endowment could be developed and implemented in future initiatives.
If we adopt the standard approach, planting will be completed early in the grant period to ensure the maintenance budget supports critical early-stage tasks such as site prep, watering, and installation. This will demonstrate tangible progress and compliance with grant requirements while ensuring readiness for subsequent implementation phases and long-term success. At the same time, Hyphae remains committed to advancing innovative stewardship strategies, such as the escrow model, to address the broader challenge of sustaining vegetative barriers beyond the grant period.
**2. <comment_start id=kix.wv0v3bhfm072>Location of Project <comment_end id=kix.wv0v3bhfm072>**
Provide address or cross-streets and describe surrounding area. Please attach photographs of the project site, and aerial photo of the project from an online resource, and a representation of the project plan area (i.e., site plan). 
As previously mentioned, t<comment_start id=kix.1hmlrqles73t>he City is presently in the design stages for curb and gutter improvements along South Comanche Drive and <comment_start id=kix.qoph4ejnb9ni>Tejon Highway<comment_end id=kix.qoph4ejnb9ni>, projects which are also partially funded through a separate AB617 grant. These are the corridors that have been identified in this RFP by the community as priority areas for vegetated barrier projects. While exact planting locations will be finalized post-award, in collaboration with City and County partners, we have identified several potential sites along Tejon Highway that align with the planned infrastructure improvements. With the available budget, only portions of the identified area can be planted.
This actually provides a unique opportunity to plant a few different configurations of buffers that can be measured and studied over an initial phase, and then later further filled in along the corridor<comment_end id=kix.1hmlrqles73t>. We therefore want to leverage this opportunity to design the project as a research study to evaluate the effectiveness of vegetative barriers in mitigating dust levels and other environmental impacts under varying site-specific conditions.
To achieve this, we propose pairing vegetative barrier planting sites with a control area located nearby, allowing for a comparative analysis of air quality and environmental improvements. We have identified areas of Tejon near Valasco Street and Sycamore Road as potential barrier sites for example. One potential barrier could be located near an orchard, where dust levels may be lower, while another site could be near an agricultural field, which may experience higher dust exposure. Each location would allow us to test the benefits of vegetated barriers under distinct environmental conditions.
Control areas will be established approximately 200–300 feet from the vegetative barrier to provide a baseline comparison. Mobile sensing equipment will collect data across the barrier and control areas at three intervals: before planting and during two rounds of post-planting monitoring. These data collection efforts will quantify the effectiveness of vegetated barriers in reducing dust levels and other pollutants over time.
To ensure scientifically sound barrier placement, Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) modeling will simulate airflow and pollutant dispersion prior to planting. Post-planting monitoring will provide data to validate and refine these models, creating a feedback loop that enhances both immediate project outcomes and the applicability of these methods for future projects. Through our preparatory work, we have confirmed with the Arvin Community Services District that unused irrigation valves are available along the community-prioritized Comanche and Tejon areas of interest, providing crucial flexibility for the study’s irrigation needs.
This research-driven project is thus designed to remain adaptive, with exact barrier locations and configurations to be finalized in collaboration with City and County partners. Adjustments will be informed by site-specific factors such as water availability, updated environmental data, and alignment with existing infrastructure improvements. Potential future expansions could include the County-owned east side of Tejon or the west side of Comanche. Additional funding opportunities may also enable the City and its partners to pursue vegetative barriers in key areas such as South A Street, Bear Mountain Elementary School, and Arvin High School. In Lamont, priority sites such as Lamont Elementary School and Lamont Park may also be considered for future barriering projects.

**4. Describe Proposed Greenery **
Include quantity of each plant or tree to be planted. Also note environmental benefits of the selected species (e.g., if the species is native, drought resistant, non-allergenic, or low-BVOC emitting), if possible.
We are leveraging native or regionally adapted, low-water-use, drought-tolerant species. Not just any species can be used, however, as specific plant characteristics and performance metrics are critical variables for vegetated barriers. Leaf area density is the primary driver of air filtration and wind dynamics. The fine needle structure of evergreens has been shown to enhance deposition. Additionally, low VOC emissions are essential for vegetated barrier effectiveness. Based on research conducted by our team in past projects, conifers offer the best pollution removal potential. We have thus included both native species and non-native, non-invasive drought-tolerant candidates, carefully selected to account for climate change projections and species migration patterns.

**6. Proposed Irrigation Systems **
Describe the type of irrigation systems (e.g., drip, overhead spray) 
<comment_start id=kix.x6113g4ti8y4>Drip irrigation will be installed for primary water supply to the plants. Additional spray irrigation will also be provided for periodic washing because it has been found that in dry climates, vegetated barriers accumulate PM and become less effective.<comment_end id=kix.x6113g4ti8y4>
**7. Project Limitations **
Describe any possible project limits such as parking, hours of operation, available staffing, user fees, seasonal restrictions, or other ecological considerations. 
Although the community has identified multiple ideal locations for vegetated buffers, some of these sites would require collaboration with private landowners and farmers. For this project, we are focusing on sites controlled by the City of Arvin. The successful implementation of this project is expected to foster further discussions and demonstrations, paving the way for future collaboration with private landowners.
Planting larger trees in the vegetated barrier is a priority, as they provide an immediate and impactful demonstration of what smaller-planted buffers could develop into over time. Larger trees also enhance the buffer's effectiveness sooner, delivering tangible benefits to the community and allowing for earlier testing of air quality and environmental improvements.
This demonstration project represents a crucial first step in establishing evidence-based insights that can guide future local and regional policy, secure funding, and enable the implementation of more vegetated barriers. These future projects would help meet the community’s desire for larger, more comprehensive barriers at a scale necessary to fully address air quality and environmental challenges in the region.
One limitation of the project stems from the challenge of selecting barrier sites early in the process. Ideally, air pollution modeling and agency coordination would inform site selection to ensure the most impactful locations are chosen. However, practical constraints often require sites to be selected before such detailed analyses can be completed.
The precise placement of trees within each site is also critical to maximizing their air pollution mitigation benefits. Tree placement will be refined during the design and modeling phases to ensure optimal performance.
Fortunately, we have the support of the City of Arvin and other stakeholders, which provides flexibility in selecting a site for the vegetative barrier. During the initial modeling phase, we will evaluate additional potential sites in collaboration with these stakeholders to refine the intervention and ensure its effectiveness.
**8. <comment_start id=kix.s89ye2tys3ja>Sensitive<comment_end id=kix.s89ye2tys3ja> Receptors **
Provide a list of sensitive receptors (e.g., schools, day cares, residences) within 1,000 feet of the project
<comment_start id=kix.5gbmktnnuv5s>Sensitive receptors in the proposed project area include a significant population of seniors and children, as highlighted by census data indicating that 9.3% of residents are below the age of five and 5.5% over the age of 65. While there are no schools or senior centers within the 1,000 feet of the corridor, this corridor has been prioritized by the community in the AB 617 planning process. Also, the entire neighborhood is classified as at-risk for poor air quality. The project area’s eastern border is adjacent to large annual crop fields. Research shows that by placing the vegetated barriers closer to the pollution source may also provide greater impact than closer to the receptors. Additionally, 31.7% of the population lives in poverty, which further underscores the vulnerability of residents to environmental health risks. These factors underscore the urgent need for targeted interventions to address air quality concerns in this area.<comment_end id=kix.5gbmktnnuv5s>
Included below is a snapshot of the <comment_start id=kix.y626yz5hbeep>sensitive receptor database we developed<comment_end id=kix.y626yz5hbeep>. We look forward to building more barriers in areas that meet the needs of these vulnerable populations.

The implementation plan will involve developing a detailed plan and project timeline for key activities such as site selection, data collection, planning, vendor coordination, equipment procurement, staff training (including creating safety protocols for both the site and equipment), as well as planting and monitoring and will include regular meetings with stakeholders.
A maintenance manual will be developed to help us raise our capacity to plant and maintain the long term health of veg. barriers planted in the grant period. Irrigation repair, canopy cleaning, and understory thinning will be included in the manual. Along with the O\&M manual, Hyphae will work together with the  Arvin/Lamont Steering Committee to develop a long-term, community-based stewardship program that includes watering, plant and tree maintenance. Regarding maintenance, w<comment_start id=kix.j5c2lhqlnqw4>e<comment_end id=kix.j5c2lhqlnqw4> have budgeted for three years of establishment maintenance and seven years of long-term maintenance. In the first three years, we have set aside $18,000 for tasks such as irrigation system checks, structural pruning, mulching, and replacement of up to 15% of trees, ensuring the vegetation establishes properly and remains healthy. These efforts will focus on intensive care to optimize tree survivorship and early growth.
For the remaining seven years of the contract, long-term maintenance will focus on periodic irrigation system checks, light pruning, and tree survivorship. The budget for this period is set at 15% of the total establishment maintenance costs ($2,700), ensuring cost-effective support tailored to the project’s evolving requirements.
Project stewardship, performance, and monitoring: During this phase Hyphae staff will train for site prep, planting, and monitoring and maintenance. Performance will be quantified with before and after data to determine the overall efficacy of the veg barriers to reduce air pollution, provide shade, and lower temps. Monitoring is a joint effort between data collected and in-the-field staff who will monitor the viability of trees responding to environmental and health issues. Hyphae will facilitate partnerships with and will work together with the Arvin/Lamont Steering Committee to pilot a stewardship program to maintain, monitor, and evaluate implemented projects, including local resident job training and hiring.
**<comment_start id=kix.xe14mr3phoow>Describe the measures that will be utilized to assure completion of the project within the indicated time.<comment_end id=kix.xe14mr3phoow>**
Hyphae will manage the Grant Agreement including compliance with grant requirements, and preparation and submission of supporting grant documents. Hyphae will prepare invoices including relevant supporting documentation for submittal and will also coordinate with partnering agencies and managing consultants/contractors. In addition, there will be ongoing participation in meetings with stakeholders and the community; development and implementation of project plans; record-keeping procedures; reporting procedures; and financial tracking and disbursements. The following deliverables will ensure the project is completed during the proposed timeline: 
  - Implementation schedule
  - Stakeholder and Arvin/Lamont Steering Committee engagement 
  - Oversee the planting in collaboration with local tree planting groups, job training, organizations, and local contractors 
  - Develop success criteria 
  - Project Monitoring Plan: monitoring protocol for air quality before, during, and after implementation 
  - Develop O\&M Manual 
  - Pilot a stewardship program to maintain, monitor, and evaluate implemented projects.

### **SECTION 3 - PROJECT INFORMATION**
#### **1. Project Summary**
x is the leading tree planting and environmental justice organization in Arvin/Lamont and has been an advocate of vegetated barriers throughout our participation in the steering committee.
Vegetated barriers require complex site analysis and selection, simulation modeling, and detailed design in order to optimize design and conditions to potentially achieve air filtration benefit. x is partnering with Hyphae Design Laboratory, a national leader in vegetated barrier design for air filtration. Hyphae has collaborated with Rich Baldauf and Max Zhang, who wrote the EPA guidelines referenced in this RFP. They are a national leader in developing novel evidence-based greening design interventions but based in Oakland and have been researching and promoting vegetated barriers and other ecosystem service-based design for almost a decade. Hyphae has contracts researching and designing vegetated barriers in Oakland, Richmond, Louisville, Dallas, and other cities. Hyphae is also committed to the scientific rigor of these systems and has grants from NIH and NSF grants to build and test different systems and monitor the water use and air filtration effectiveness and have developed protocols and instrumentation and design standards.
Hyphae will conduct fluid dynamics modeling of air and pollution at the proposed location and alternatives to optimize the design of the vegetated barrier intervention.
Additionally, we have forged a relationship with Caltrans to feature vegetated barriers on their right of way. Finally, our ongoing partnership with Asa Bradman from UC Merced will allow us to study the before and after effects of the barriers.
Based on the iTree Report, our proposed project would sequester 2,664,186 pounds of CO2. It would remove 3,553 pounds of O3, 530 pounds of NO2, 133 pounds of SO2, and 172 pounds of PM 2.5.
#### **2. Location of Project**
Proposed location is along \[specific highway/intersection in Arvin/Lamont\]. Caltrans has shown interest in revitalizing this corridor that cuts through the community and improving the adjacent land for vegetated barriers as well as park use and access. Additional vegetated barrier locations are being vetted with Caltrans that will also be considered and studied during the initial site optimization stage.
#### **3. Is the applicant the property owner of the land on which the project will be implemented?**
  - ☑ No.
  - The plantings will occur in our project partner, Caltrans Right of Way. See letter of support.
#### **4. Describe Proposed Greenery**
While native and drought-tolerant species are a goal for planting throughout the community in all our urban greening work, specific plant characteristics and performance are critical variables for vegetated barriers. Leaf area density is the primary driver of air filtration and wind dynamics. The fine needle shape of evergreens has been shown to increase deposition. Additionally, low VOC requirements are critical for vegetated barrier effectiveness. Because conifers, which research that our team has conducted on past projects indicates have the best pollution disruption potential, do not have as many low water California native options, this list includes both natives and some non-invasive drought-tolerant candidates.
**Plant List:**
  - Calocedrus decurrens (Incense Cedar)
  - Cedrus deodara (Deodar Cedar)
  - Chamaecyparis lawsoniana (Port Orford Cedar)
  - Cupressus arizonica (Arizona Cypress)
  - Cupressus sempervirens (Italian Cypress)
  - Juniperus californica (California Juniper)
  - Juniperus chinensis (Chinese Juniper)
  - Pinus canariensis (Canary Island Pine)
  - Pinus pinea (Italian Stone Pine)
  - Pseudotsuga menziesii (Douglas Fir)
  - Sequoia sempervirens (Redwood)
  - Thuja plicata (Western Red Cedar)
#### **5. Area of Project Footprint to Be Created or Enhanced**
67,921 SF
#### **6. Proposed Irrigation Systems**
Drip irrigation will be installed for primary water supply to the plants. Additional spray irrigation will also be provided for periodic washing because it has been found that in dry climates, vegetated barriers accumulate PM and become less effective.
#### **7. Project Limitations**
One project limitation is the dichotomy between needing to choose sites and needing to do air pollution modeling to assess the performance of vegetated barriers sites that are optimal to see the benefits of vegetated barriers. Additionally, the precise placement of trees affects this performance and will need to be optimized during design and modeling. In the future, preliminary work being done for the AB617 wide prioritization plan will help streamline this in the future. For this grant, we have gained approval for a priority site, but during the initial modeling phase, we plan on studying additional sites with Caltrans to implement the most effective intervention and site.
#### **8. Sensitive Receptors**
Included above is a snapshot of the sensitive receptor database we developed. Access can be provided. A few of the closest receptors include Spanos Elementary (816ft), Hazel Elementary, and Jubilee Adult Day Program (17ft).

# Scraps

These corridors have also been identified by the community as priority areas for vegetated barrier projects. into their planned infrastructure work. 
While exact planting locations will be finalized post-award in collaboration with City and County partners, we have identified several potential areas along Tejon Highway that align with these infrastructure improvements. 
With the available budget only portions of the identified area can be planted.
The City is presently in the design stages for curb and gutter improvements along the community-prioritized section of Tejon Highway and has agreed, in its letter of support, to integrate our vegetated barrier project into their planned infrastructure work. To evaluate barrier performance under differing environmental conditions, we propose building two distinct barriers: the first located just south of Valasco Street and the second further up the block, halfway between Valasco and Sycamore Road. The former barrier is located across from an orchard whereas the latter stands opposite from an agricultural field. The two barriers, comprising a total of 132 trees, will thus allow us to study the benefits of vegetated barriers in reducing dust levels under differing site-specific conditions, with the orchard-adjacent barrier expected to encounter less dust and the agricultural field barrier contending with significantly higher dust exposure. 
In addition to the two barriers, we are siting control areas 225 feet north and south of the respective barrier locations to serve as baselines for comparison. We have budgeted for mobile sensing across both the barriers and the control areas, with one round of data collected prior to planting and two additional rounds collected post-planting. This approach will enable us to quantify and compare the effectiveness of the vegetated barriers in mitigating dust levels and other environmental factors over time, providing robust data to inform future interventions.
Prior to planting, we will conduct Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) modeling to assess site conditions. This initial modeling will simulate airflow and pollutant dispersion in the project area, providing a baseline understanding of environmental conditions and informing the exact placement and configuration of the vegetative barriers. Following planting, we will compare the CFD predictions with monitoring data collected from both the barrier sites and the control areas. This iterative approach allows us to evaluate the barriers’ performance in reducing dust and air pollution and refine the modeling process for future applications.
Looking ahead, Hyphae is available to assist the City and other stakeholders with strategizing and master planning for future barriers. Additional funding opportunities could be pursued to expand the scope of this project to other community-identified areas of interest, such as the County-owned west side of Comanche. Future funding rounds could also support barriering projects in several community-prioritized areas. These include the upcoming AB 617-funded City project on South A Street, the playfields at Bear Mountain Elementary School to mitigate crop drift, and key locations such as the new community college campus and Arvin High School. In Lamont, additional opportunities exist at Lamont Elementary School and Lamont Park, both identified by the community as areas of interest.

The City is presently in the design stages for curb and gutter improvements along a community-prioritized section of Tejon Highway and has expressed support for integrating a vegetated barrier project into their planned infrastructure work. While exact planting locations will be finalized post-award in collaboration with City and County partners, we have identified several potential areas along Tejon Highway that align with these infrastructure improvements.
To explore barrier performance under varying environmental conditions, we aim to install two distinct barriers within this corridor. A preliminary study has identified areas near Valasco Street and Sycamore Road as potential barrier sites. One potential barrier could be located near an orchard, where dust levels may be lower, while another site could be near an agricultural field, which may experience higher dust exposure. Together, these locations would allow us to study the benefits of vegetated barriers under different conditions.
Control areas will be selected within approximately 200–300 feet of the vegetated barriers to establish baseline comparisons. Mobile sensing equipment will be used to collect data across the barriers and control areas at three intervals: before planting and during two rounds of post-planting monitoring. This data will support a comparative analysis of vegetative barrier effectiveness in mitigating dust levels and other environmental impacts.
To ensure optimal barrier placement, Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) modeling will be conducted before planting. This modeling will simulate airflow and pollutant dispersion to guide site-specific configurations. Post-planting monitoring will provide data to validate and refine these models, enhancing their applicability for future barrier designs. Through our preparatory work, we have confirmed with the Arvin Community Services District that unused irrigation valves are available along the community-prioritized Comanche and Tejon areas of interest, providing crucial flexibility for the project’s irrigation requirements.
This project is designed to remain adaptive, with exact barrier locations and configurations to be finalized in collaboration with City and County partners. Adjustments will be informed by site-specific factors such as water availability, updated environmental data, and alignment with existing infrastructure improvements. Potential future expansions could include the County-owned west side of Comanche or other community-identified areas. Additional funding opportunities may enable the City and its partners to pursue vegetative barriers in key areas such as South A Street, Bear Mountain Elementary School, and Arvin High School. In Lamont, priority sites such as Lamont Elementary School and Lamont Park may also be considered for future barriering projects.

“The following communityled microclimate observatory and task force is designed to mitigate extreme heat risk in the Central and imperial valleys, and then to expand to other regions of the state in future rounds of funding. ComMON proposes installing a thermal sensor network and pilot greening interventions to support an agentbased heat mitigation app for community members most afflicted by extreme heat. This is integrated with health data studies to validate the efficacy of the program.”

Material from EHCRP:
Section entitled: Hyphae integrates aerial, ground-level, and public health data  
Description
HDL facilitates integration of LMR, VIP, CCV, and Leap data collection effort with ongoing integration of UC Merced’s and JPL’s ECOSTRESS data sets. Hyphae, UCM and JPL will perform statistical analysis on thermal imagery data, ground sensor data, other remote sensing data, and the health data from local hospitals. This will include pre-post studies of the built-environment interventions, and, where applicable, cross-sectional analysis of built environment features.
Deliverables/Milestones
As sensor data is generated, the resulting data points (on radiant temperature, wind speed, air temperature, humidity, and activity spaces) will be mapped in a spatial time-series database, which will also ingest JPL and UC Merced spatial data on surface temperature and health outcomes/hospital admissions respectively. As the data is populated, the integrated dataset will be used to provide actionable insights for the intervention process. The data will be used as inputs for simulations of sun, shade, and re-radiation of heat from materials. These simulations will be used to estimate heat stress in chosen places and times, and to determine optimal activity spaces (coolest routes at each time of day per season). Surface temperature data and local sensor data will be combined with heat-stress indices to develop a comprehensive method for estimating heat-stress exposure of locations at different times of day and year. This will be combined with health data from local hospitals to estimate the impacts of heat on health outcomes and how much those impacts have been mitigated by the completed built-environment interventions in task 5.  
Section entitled: Hyphae gathers spatial data for the digital twin
Description
HDL gathers aerial LiDAR date for chosen neighborhoods in preparation for modeling digital twins that represent the area's heat stress in hyperlocal detail.  
Deliverables/Milestones
This results in 3 dimensional spatial data on terrain, buildings and vegetation, with leaf area density and land cover. This data will be ground-truthed and corrected by community members.
Section entitled: Hyphae prepares a digital twin and runs simulations
Description
Combining multiple layers of data: JPL’s aerial imagery, and the ground-level monitoring done by LMR, VIP, CCV, and Leap, HDL designs a digital twin of the chosen neighborhoods using the AdaptOS platform. For each of the 8 communities, a digital twin (a 3D computer model of buildings, vegetation and terrain) will be created. The digital twin will be run through environmental simulations of wind flow and sunlight paths to create estimates of universal thermal comfort index (UTCI) for the entire space on representative hot summer days and nights, and dynamic physiological equivalent temperature (dPET)  for common pedestrian routes identified by the community. The models will then be modified to represent proposed interventions on the built environment by the community. These proposed intervention's will then be run through the same simulations, and the relative merits of different design decisions (in the heat stress context) will be quantified, compared and contrasted to facilitate evidence-based design optimization. 
Deliverables/Milestones
The major outcomes of this activity will be 3 dimensional models of the terrain, depicting buildings and vegetation of the targeted neighborhoods at at meter scale, and subsequent simulation results. Meter-scale land cover analyis as well as three-dimensional leaf area density estimations will provide a digital twin that can be used for computational fluid dynamics simulations of wind flow, as well as radiation simulations of thermal absorption and emissions. Various design iterations developed by the community-driven design process will be subjected to environmental simulations, which will provide metrics of their performance for heat-stress mitigation. This will enable the community to make evidence-based design decisions and create interventions that are maximally effective or have the greatest ratio of benefit to cost.
Section entitled “Hyphae designs an extreme heat related app or outreach tool” (Good material on UFMP)
Description
“HDL designs a routing and outreach app for extreme heat events. Our proposal for the app, subject to community review, is as follows. It first alerts residents about imminent extreme heat events. Then, during the heat wave itself, the app generates shade maps that guide users down the coolest routes to their destination: schools, worksites, community cooling centers, and other sensitive receptor sites. App design will use existing geospatial and user-interface libraries to enable users to leverage project database and backend algorithms for making use of sensor data, remote sensing data, modeling & simulation results, as well as network analysis tools. The database will be populated with sensor data, remote sensing metrics, and simulation results. Meanwhile, the backend algorithms will use network analysis to provide the user with routes optimized for thermal comfort based on the user's inputs of origins, destinations, outdoor activity type, and location data.”
Deliverables/Milestones
Development on the app begins Day 1, but with increasingly sophisticated versions deployed from months 12 - 30 as community needs are refined and integrated into app design. The app is designed to alert the community in the event of an extreme heat event, proposing optimal routes to sensitive receptor sites such as schools, community centers, churches, and designated cooling centers. This will include metrics of the cooling potential of existing and proposed greenspace, shade, wind, and facilities, and will use network analytics to direct people to utilize paths and locations with the minimal possible heat stress.  The simulations behind the tool will use local weather forecasts to provide forcing inputs for simulations of heat stress that take advantage of 3 dimensional digital twins of the area. The digital twins are put through simulation process based on the Urban Multi-scale Environmental Predictor (UMEP) tool, which calculates meter-scale mean radiant temperature and wind-velocity, while taking into consideration  time of day and year, the effects of buildings, vegetation, terrain and material properties on the wind flow,  sunlight and radiation. The results of the simulation are meter-scale maps of hot and cool zones, which are then fed into a network analysis algorithm that calculates the shortest path weighted by heat-stress between an origin and destination.  The app will enable community members to minimize their cumulative heat exposure by helping to plan routes and timing of outdoor activities to favor the lowest heat-exposing decisions.  These simulations are computationally intensive, and so will only be available in selected pilot neighborhoods at first. During this pilot period, other neighborhoods will still be able to use the tool, but with a less computationally intensive model, which uses heuristics based on Google-Streetview derived view indices, which calculate skyview index and shade cover by analysis of low-cost Google-Streetview imagery. In both cases, the routing recommendations are based on simulations or heuristics, which present an ethical concern if not backed-up by validation and ground truthing. We will use community deployed sensor networks, and remote sensing thermal imaging to validate and ground truth the simulations and heuristics. These methods of the modeling and validation cycle will then be subject to peer review in the scientific community to ensure validity and rigor. After a period of usage of the app, it will then be possible to discern its impact on health outcomes using hospital admissions data analyzed by the University of Merced affiliated team members.

## A. Transmittal Letter

## C. Overview
1\. Project Understanding
  - Fresno County Climate Context
  - Historical data showing 100+ days over 100°F in 2023
  - Projected increases in extreme heat days under climate scenarios
  - Disproportionate impacts on disadvantaged communities (55% of census tracts above 75th percentile on CalEnviroScreen 4.0)

  - Heat vulnerability assessment methodology
  - Community engagement strategy

C. Overview
Fresno County faces an unprecedented challenge from extreme heat that disproportionately affects its most vulnerable residents. In 2023 alone, the county experienced more than 100 days where temperatures exceeded 100°F. Climate projections indicate this trend will intensify  the annual average maximum temperature is expected to rise by 5.3°F under a medium emission scenario, or 8.7°F under a high emission scenario by century's end. The number of extreme heat days (above 94.3°F) is projected to increase from 5 days per year to 43 days under a medium emissions scenario, and to 66 days under a high emissions scenario.
These impacts are not distributed equally. Over 55% of census tracts in Fresno County rank above the 75th percentile on CalEnviroScreen 4.0, indicating communities burdened by multiple environmental challenges including poverty, air pollution, and health inequities. These factors make residents particularly vulnerable to heatrelated illnesses. Many residents lack adequate cooling resources  the average income is 25% below the state average, and over 58% live in lowincome communities where residents may struggle to afford cooling units or adequate housing insulation.
Our approach to the Fresno County Extreme Heat Analysis recognizes that effective planning requires both rigorous technical analysis and deep community engagement. While existing heat awareness tools provide general guidelines with broad countylevel data, they often lack the granularity needed for targeted interventions. Tools like CalAdapt use downscaled climate projections at a 3mile resolution  sometimes representing entire cities as single data points. Our analysis will provide the highresolution data needed to identify vulnerable hotspots and design effective mitigation strategies.
1\. Project Understanding
Fresno County faces escalating extreme heat challenges that disproportionately affect vulnerable residents. In 2023, the county experienced over 100 days exceeding 100°F. Climate projections show annual average maximum temperatures rising 5.38.7°F by century's end, with extreme heat days (\>94.3°F) increasing from 5 to 4366 days annually under different emissions scenarios.
These impacts fall hardest on disadvantaged communities  over 55% of county census tracts rank above the 75th percentile on CalEnviroScreen 4.0. With average incomes 25% below state levels and 58% in lowincome areas, many residents struggle to afford adequate cooling or housing insulation.
2\. Project Organization
Our team brings together experts in:
 Climate science and heat vulnerability assessment
 Community engagement and environmental justice
 Urban forestry and green infrastructure
 GIS and data visualization
We will work closely with the Fresno COG Project Manager and a Working Group representing local jurisdictions and community organizations.
3\. Technical Approach Summary  
Our methodology combines high resolution heat vulnerability assessment with robust community engagement:
Heat Assessment & Data Collection
 Integration of satellite temperature data, local climate records, and CalEnviroScreen indicators
 Advanced environmental simulations analyzing urban heat island effects and thermal comfort metrics
 Highresolution mapping of current tree canopy and vulnerable areas
Community Engagement
 Formation of diverse Working Group representing affected communities
 Multilingual outreach in English and Spanish
 Interactive workshops combining technical information with local knowledge
 Continuous feedback loop between analysis and community input
The resulting analysis will provide actionable data to guide future tree canopy and vegetative cover projects that maximize heat mitigation benefits for vulnerable communities.
A biophysical approach to heat
High resolution population location data
Increased exposure
People who work outdoors, are experiencing homelessness, or are emergency responders are at higher risk. 
Sensitive receptors, People who are under 5, over 65, pregnant, or have chronic health conditions are more vulnerable. This work is normally limited by census tract level and far worse in dispersed rural areas. 
Through ongoing collaboration with UC Merced.
Socioeconomic burden
People who are low income, live in low resource communities, or have disabilities are more vulnerable.
 
Lack of access to cooling
People who don't have access to or can't afford air conditioning are more susceptible. 
Historic underinvestment
Communities of color and low-income communities have less greenspace and tree planting, which contributes to hotter urban temperatures.

### From CARB SMMI PEG Application
What relevant organization or agency are you affiliated with? What is your role or title there?
Brent Bucknum is the Principal and Founder of Hyphae Design Lab. I sit on the West Oakland AB 617 steering committee. Our firm has over fifteen years of air quality partnerships with environmental justice organizations and we were instrumental in founding West Oakland as one of the first AB 617 communities with the West Oakland Environmental Indicators Project (WOEIP). \[Not sure how widely we should be interpreting “affiliation” with “relevant organization or agency” here\]    
What geographic area or region are you most familiar with? Please describe your relationship with the area or region.
Though we work with community groups in Stockton, Boyle Heights, Richmond, and elsewhere, we have done most of our work in our home of West Oakland. \[Expand. Not sure what’s best to say here in terms of our reach vs. our specialization\] 
Applicants should have experience in one or more of the following areas: 
  - Air Quality Monitoring & Data Analysis: Experience with air quality monitoring technologies, mobile data platforms, or environmental health analysis. 
  - Community Engagement: Expertise in working with disadvantaged or vulnerable communities, particularly in environmental justice efforts. 
  - Public Policy or Regulatory Affairs: Background in advising or influencing public policy decisions at the regional, state, or national levels. 
  - Environmental Science or Engineering: Academic or practical experience in environmental science, engineering, or related technical fields. 
  - Public Health: Knowledge of public health principles, particularly related to air quality impacts and community health. 
Describe your research or practical experience related to air quality monitoring, public health, or integrating community experience into scientific research or policy development. Describe additional skills and/or expertise as they relate to the focus and purpose of the SMMI and PEG. 
I have experience in public air quality monitoring and modeling, community engagement, environmental engineering, as well as public health and medical research. Since 2009, I have worked on environmental justice and air quality issues in West Oakland and now serve as a Steering Committee member for West Oakland’s AB 617 committee. As a technical consultant, assist communities in Richmond, Boyle Heights, East Los Angeles, Stockton, Marin City, and other groups across the country. My work with mobile air monitoring data and translation of that data to community members dates back to the first EDF–Google–Josh Apte project on West Oakland (2017). We have experience analyzing, filtering, and using data for planning purposes in Oakland, Boyle Heights, and across the Bay area. I was also a founding member of the Greenheart Louisville Project and led the greening team core in the first ever urban greening interventional trial funded by the NIH in Louisville, Kentucky. In that project, I collaborated with Environmental Monitoring specialist Jay Turner from Washington University in Saint Louis to utilize mobile ultrafine particle monitoring data for greening intervention design.
While I am applying as myself, I will also bring the knowledge and experience of the rest of the Hyphae team: Daniel Fleischer (Chief Scientist), Ivan Heitmann, (Design and Modeling Specialist), Juan Penaloza Gutierrez PhD (CFD Modeling Expert), and Mei Visco (Ecological Designer and Project Manager of numerous AB 617 grants).
Hyphae brings together diverse stakeholders, from community-based organizations (CBOs) to academic researchers, regulatory agencies, and city governments into a solution-oriented, collaborative framework of environmental remediation. Our team of data scientists, community liaisons, urban planners, civil and environmental engineers, and public health experts ensure that the data we collect is used not only for analysis but also to develop tangible interventions that address specific environmental and public health challenges. 
Our process begins with hyperlocal mapping of the built environment. We then measure and model how a given solution might impact environmental and public health outcomes. These models allow communities to visualize environmental risks, make informed decisions about interventions, and determine the cost-benefit trade-offs of different solutions, ensuring that each option is carefully evaluated for its effectiveness and impact before implementation. Our solutions range from vegetated buffers, urban greening, truck rerouting, and road diets, as well as supporting our partners in advocating for policy recommendations.
The Statewide Mobile Monitoring Initiative (SMMI) builds on AB 617’s mandate for community-led air quality monitoring and emissions reduction. Long before AB 617 was signed into law, however, Hyphae had been practicing this collaborative, evidence-based form of environmental justice advocacy. Our work with current and soon-to-be AB 617 communities continues in this tradition, leveraging our institutional knowledge, technical expertise, and long-standing relationships with CBOs, regulatory agencies, and local governments. This legacy makes us invaluable partners in the Project Expert Group (PEG), ensuring SMMI's goals are met through effective, community-driven interventions.
\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_
What are your interests and experiences related to air quality in your community or more broadly in California? How do you see these interests and experiences contributing to the PEG? 
*You may also submit a resume along with your application.   *
*Your answer*
We believe that for air quality remediation to be effective, it cannot rely on census tract information exclusively. More granular data and models are needed for site-specific surveys and interventions. With rising temperatures compounding the effects of air pollution and amplifying health risks, even as overall air quality improves, such a hyperlocal approach will only become more urgent.
Our long-term vision for air quality in the state is to integrate environmental health into epidemiology. We believe our research and design tools can help shift the current reactive healthcare model to a preventative health framework that directs public and clinical health funding to community scale infrastructure interventions. By re-designing cities to mitigate environmental stressors—such as air pollution, heat, and inadequate infrastructure—we can address the root causes of illness before they impact public health, fostering healthier communities and more resilient urban environments. To support this, we have developed software tools that automate processes that formerly required costly teams of landscape architects and consultants, making climate adaptation and public health interventions more accessible and scalable for communities.
Our contribution to the PEG will focus on refining and expanding the partnerships we've built, continuing to weave together community-based activism with academic research and the work of local government. When it comes to data gathering, we are committed to moving beyond census tracts as the sole unit of analysis. More granular data-sets allow communities to better understand local environmental conditions, make informed decisions, and implement targeted interventions that address their specific challenges. We further hope that California moves towards a public health framework that fully integrates environmental risk factors. Agencies such as CARB can help shift the current reactive healthcare model to a more preventative approach by integrating environmental data into public health strategies, addressing issues like air pollution and heat before they impact the health of frontline communities. Just as California has led the country in environmental innovation with initiatives such as CalEnviroScreen, AirNow, and Cal-Adapt, this shift toward more granular data and integrating environmental factors into public health frameworks will ensure California remains at the forefront of climate adaptation and public health advancements.

\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_
Why do you want to serve on the PEG? What will be your unique contribution?
*Your answer*
My work is both my passion and a form of community service. I want to share my experience from working in my own community at the intersection of environmental engineering, community engagement, academia, and regulatory agencies. I aim to help other communities facing similar challenges and to improve communication between agencies, technical experts, and those communities.
I developed a class called "EJ 101 for Engineers," which Margaret Gordon from WOEIP has engineers and scientists from the air district and other partners go through before engaging with community groups. I bring a pragmatic, science-based perspective and promote an evidence-based design model. My goal is to translate measurement and modeling into real-world interventions. I can help engineers, academics, and community members understand what monitoring data can and cannot achieve, and explain when and where different types of monitoring equipment—be they low-cost or more sensitive instruments—are most effective.
As a steering committee member, I’ve seen the frustration that arises when data isn’t translated into action. I’ve also witnessed communities, like my own, develop long lists of proposals, only to be paralyzed by indecision due to inadequate models, uncertainty about effectiveness, and a lack of cost-benefit analysis. I want to help other communities overcome these obstacles and turn data into meaningful solutions.
### NSF Material from previous grants:
#### From CMEJ-MPCA grant:
Hyphae Design Lab is a national leader in evidence-based design. In partnership with communities and academics, they have received funding from the National Science Foundation and the National Institutes of Health to develop an adaptive management platform named adaptOS. This software-enabled platform synthesizes aerial, satellite, and ground-level data to create high-resolution, hyperlocal maps that model local risks to air, water, and soil. In consultation with communities, the platform can then generate adaptive environmental design interventions to maximize human health and biodiversity, as well as mitigate other threats such as extreme heat and flood risk. With the University of Louisville, Hyphae is presently leading the first and largest clinical trial designed to uncover the effects of urban greening on air quality at a neighborhood scale. 
#### From CMEJ Google Data Grant:
In collaboration with Hyphae Design Lab, we propose a data-driven environmental justice project that integrates citizen science and community capacity-building. Our initiative will capture and visualize hyperlocal air quality data across our AOIs by deploying a network of fixed and mobile air quality sensors. These sensors will be installed and maintained by a cohort of trained citizen scientists. The data collected will be transformed into high-resolution computational fluid dynamics (CFD) models to simulate how pollution disperses through our community and identify mitigation strategies. Potential solutions, such as vegetated buffers, road diets, and reforested industrial corridors, will not only enhance air quality but also help cool the environment, addressing the compounding effects of pollution and rising temperatures. This data-driven approach will empower our community to advocate effectively for equitable environmental policies and help us secure additional funding in future implementation-focused grant opportunities.
We selected Hyphae as our technical partners for their expertise in leveraging advanced technology to support community-based organizations (CBOs) fighting environmental racism. Partnering with leading scientific institutions and academic researchers, Hyphae equips grassroots organizations with the data and tools needed to drive meaningful environmental change. In 2020, they received National Science Foundation funding to develop adaptOS, a platform that synthesizes aerial, satellite, and ground-level data into high-resolution environmental models, enabling communities to design and implement data-driven interventions. Hyphae is currently leading a clinical trial with the University of Louisville to study the [effects of urban greening](https://www.nbcnews.com/health/heart-health/living-tree-filled-areas-may-reduce-heart-disease-risk-study-shows-rcna168214) on air quality and is working with Climate Resolve and NASA’s Jet Propulsion Lab in Los Angeles on a [de-paving plan](https://www.latimes.com/california/story/2023-09-08/heat-waves-los-angeles-reflective-coating-streets-cool-pavement) to reduce urban heat island effects and pollution. Similar projects are underway in West Oakland, Stockton, Richmond, Marin City, and beyond.
#### From Boyle Heights Community Change:
Max’s text: 
Using hyperlocal mapping and measurement tools developed with funding from the National Science Foundation and the National Institute of Health, Hyphae helps CBOs identify air, soil, and water contamination in their communities, as well as other risks to human health such as extreme heat, flood risk, noise pollution, wildfires, and vector-borne diseases. Partners can then deploy Hyphae’s software-based adaptOS platform to simulate, design, and implement environmental mitigation solutions in response. Such interventions range from urban greening to truck-rerouting to the design of software applications protecting sensitive receptors during extreme heat events. 
The adaptOS platform iteratively cycles through a five-stage process of measurement, simulation, implementation, assessment, and refinement. After mapping a given geographical area, the platform models potential mitigation scenarios prior to construction. Once design solutions are implemented, the same tools are used to evaluate and assess the net environmental impact of those designs, providing empirical evidence of their effectiveness and sustainability, and allowing for corrections as needed. 
Brent’s text:
The community-led design and implementation process is also grounded in an evidence-based, scientific,  adaptive management framework. Evidence-Based design utilizes scientific basis for community adaptation. It goes beyond doing research with or for a community, which can often occur adjacent to the community empowerment work they are doing to embedding scientific within our community design decisions. 
In the Adapt evidence based design process, we do analysis and design, then do measurement and modeling to inform the design, prioritize implementation locations and measure their effects. It also de-imperializes science, as a tool that can be used by all, and not subject to academia and PHDs. Our core philosophy is in fact that community members are often the best scientists, as they inherently experience, observe and evaluate their community more than academics. 
#### From Endless Frontier (submitted 8/24)
Q: Describe what your company does in 100 words or less:
A: Hyphae Design Laboratory is an environmental engineering firm operating at the intersection of research and
design, focused on environmental health and climate adaptation. Our platform, adaptOS, revolutionizes green
infrastructure projects by integrating high-resolution environmental analytics, automated design, and project
management tools. Leveraging advanced machine learning, adaptOS analyzes data from various sources to
generate actionable insights for improving the quality of the air, water, and soil, as well as mitigating flooding and
heat islands. By combining data-driven design and management, adaptOS makes green infrastructure more
accessible and impactful, delivering measurable health and environmental benefits to communities.
Q: What is your company's long-term vision and why is it important to you as a founder?
A: Starting with our short-term ambitions to transform built environments in support of environmental health, Hyphae's
long-term vision is to integrate environmental health into epidemiology. We hope that our tools can help shift our
society’s reactive healthcare paradigm towards preventative environmental interventions in the built environment,
where illness can be prevented before it occurs. Such a holistic approach would address not only genetic but also
environmental causes of illness, leading to more effective prevention and treatment strategies in a world of healthy
Cities.
This vision of a built environment that sustains human health was shaped by my experiences in the West Oakland
environmental justice movement. Witnessing the unfair pollution burdens placed on industrial fenceline
communities, I realized how reversing these trends at a local level could be scaled up to support health and
sustainability at regional and national levels.
Q: Describe the customer pain point you are trying to solve. Why was it not solved earlier?
A: Cities are complex heterogeneous ecosystems, and low resolution data can miss critical detail that dramatically
impacts ecosystem service value and health. A difference of one single block in an implementation plan can have
dramatic differences.
Despite these health impacts of our built environment decisions, the vast majority of clients, cities, agencies and
regulators lack a coordinated comprehensive approach to much of our urban planning, and don't require
businesses, urban planners, designers and engineers to do quantifiable, evidence-based design for anything
related to ecosystem services, let alone air quality and health. When an agency requires environmental review for a
high-profile project, the assessment doesn't actually significantly impact or shift design, but rather, the review is only
for regulatory compliance. None of these reviews consider other critical human environmental health factors like risk
and exposure assessment at either the project or master planning level.
Q: Describe your target customers \[For therapeutic solutions, specify target indication(s).\]
A: Our first target customers are municipalities, regulatory agencies, environmental consultants, urban planners, and
community-based organizations. Assisting them to focus on improving environmental health outcomes through
strategic urban planning and green infrastructure projects. These customers seek data-driven solutions to meet
environmental standards, public health goals, and sustainable urban development. They need high-resolution
analytics, design tools, and evidence-based interventions to advocate for and implement changes.
For therapeutic solutions, we target air quality improvement, heat island mitigation, and pollution reduction. Our
adaptOS platform provides high-resolution environmental data, advanced modeling, and actionable insights for
effective interventions. By addressing these areas, we enhance community health, reduce disease prevalence,
and promote sustainability. Our scalable and adaptable solutions meet diverse urban planning and environmental
health needs.
Q: Describe your company's product or service and what it does, or will do.
A: Hyphae Design Laboratory's adaptOS is a green infrastructure operating system that provides a comprehensive,
end-to-end project delivery process. It integrates environmental analytics, design, implementation, monitoring, and
evaluation in one platform. Using advanced machine learning, adaptOS analyzes data from street-view images,
satellite data, and meteorological information to produce high-resolution estimates of environmental conditions.
This allows for strategic, site-specific interventions that improve community health outcomes. By bridging the gap
between data and actionable solutions, adaptOS enables municipalities, regulatory agencies, and community
organizations to implement effective and efficient environmental health initiatives. Its holistic approach addresses
both genetic and environmental factors of health, promoting preventative measures and sustainable urban
Development.
Q: Please highlight how your technology is novel, differentiated and/or transformational.
A: Our adaptOS platform offers a comprehensive, end-to-end solution for environmental health that sets it apart.
Unlike competitors, adaptOS integrates high-resolution environmental analytics, design, implementation,
monitoring, and evaluation in a single platform. It uses advanced machine learning to analyze street-view images,
satellite data, and meteorological information, producing precise environmental condition estimates. This allows for
strategic, site-specific interventions that traditional methods cannot match.
The novelty of adaptOS lies in its ability to bridge the gap between data and actionable interventions, ensuring
projects deliver measurable health outcomes. Its transformational impact is evident in how it shifts urban planning
from reactive to preventative, addressing both genetic and environmental health factors. By combining automation
and evidence-based design, adaptOS not only identifies environmental risks but also implements and monitors
effective solutions, promoting healthier communities and sustainable development.
Q: How have you tested or validated your scientific or technological innovation?
A: We have tested and validated our adaptOS platform through various projects. In Oakland, our original vision,
adaptOS provided high-resolution environmental condition estimates, leading to improved urban planning and
community health outcomes.
In Louisville, the Green Heart project marked the first NIH funded clinical trial with urban greening interventions,
demonstrating adaptOS's ability to deliver precise, site-specific interventions in partnership with the University of
Louisville. Our work on extreme heat in Dallas showcased the platform's effectiveness in mitigating urban heat
islands and enhancing public health. Additionally, our ongoing work in Boyle Heights continues to validate
adaptOS's capabilities in real-world settings, providing actionable insights for urban planning and green
infrastructure projects.
These projects, backed by measurable health and environmental improvements, confirm adaptOS's effectiveness
and scalability. Continuous monitoring and feedback from these initiatives have further refined and validated our
platform's capabilities.
Q: Provide details about any tests you have conducted to prove out your technology/science or evidence of
validation.
A: We have tested and validated our adaptOS platform through pilot projects with municipalities and community
organizations. Using advanced machine learning, adaptOS analyzed street-view images, satellite data, and
meteorological information to produce high-resolution environmental condition estimates. These projects
demonstrated adaptOS's ability to deliver precise, site-specific interventions that improve community health
outcomes.
Our validation included collaborations with partners like the Texas Trees Foundation and environmental justice
groups in Oakland and Richmond, where adaptOS provided actionable insights for urban planning and green
infrastructure projects. The success of these pilots, backed by measurable health and environmental
improvements, confirms adaptOS's effectiveness and scalability. Continuous monitoring and feedback from these
projects have further refined and validated our platform's capabilities.
Q: What major obstacles might threaten the success of your product or technology over the next 6 months?
A: We have a base consulting business that is stable and has been for a decade. The development of this platform
has been incubated for 18 months with seed funding from NSF. One major obstacle threatening the success of our
adaptOS platform over the next six months is the need for additional funding to extend our runway. Gaining
business support and advice through your program is critical to us at this point as we explore various business
models for the spin out. We are now trying to evaluate if we should raise venture capital and what type and at what
valuation. We are also concerned by the profit returns venture wants and that it could compromise our mission
driven focus and intersted in impact investing or continue to bootstrap. Currently, we are a bootstrap organization,
while we are developing new leads focused on our tools, much of this work is through grants in partnership with
nonprofits and government agencies, grant cycles and infrastructure, often taking 1-2 years. We are also
concerned that as out product is used and gets into the wild, others with more cash may catch up.
Q: Describe any commercial progress or traction you have accomplished to date.
Include any accomplishments such as partnerships, paid pilots, awards, media coverage, etc.
A: Pilot Projects are described elsewhere. We have had media coverage from Washington Post last year about our
tools and our clients have gotten press for our work(thats one of problems\!) OUr partners an clients often take the
credit for our work and collaboration. We recently hired an Outreach, PR and Coms person to get more press in our
direction.
We helped conceive of and launch this greenheart project but partners at U ofL an TNC take the credit.

Q: What is the size of your target market?
Please specify separately, where possible, your TAM, SAM, SOM
A: We have a much longer commercialization plan we would be happy to provide that goes far deeper, developed for
our NSF funding. Our niche market is global nature-based solutions for air quality. An OECD working study found
that in 2015 outdoor air pollution alone was responsible for $4.76 trillion in health care costs per year in the
combined 41 OECD and BRIICS countries, with 50% of this coming from traffic sourced pollution (Roy & Axel
Braathen, 2017). The annual costs of global outdoor air-pollution based on individual willingness-to-pay for
averting premature death are projected to expand to $25 trillion by 2060 (OECD, 2016).. The green technology
and sustainability market that our proposed platform expands us into is expected to grow from $8.7B in 2019 to
$28.9B in 2024, at a compound annual growth rate of 27.1% (Comserve, 2021). Our Serviceable Available Market
(SAM) is US-based air quality stakeholders who are innovators and currently interested in improving air quality in
their communities, although we plan to expand beyond the US. We chose this beachhead for several reasons:
Q:Why do you think your team, including your advisors, is best qualified to succeed in your current business?
Please consider the unique skill sets each person brings, such as business acumen, domain knowledge, and
operational experience.
A: Our team is uniquely qualified due to our deep industry experience and mission-driven foundation in the
environmental justice (EJ) community. With 15 years in the EJ, science, and AEC industries, we bring extensive
domain knowledge and operational experience. We've integrated software development by hiring skilled
developers, ensuring a blend of technical expertise and real-world problem-solving. Our advisors contribute
valuable insights and guidance. Unlike external software developers, we understand the intricacies of the issues
we tackle, having lived and worked within the communities we serve. This combination of skills and experience
makes us exceptionally well-equipped to succeed.
Q: Please list any expert advisors and the specific expertise they bring to your team. Please include LinkedIn, if
applicable.
A: We have scientific advisors from various academic institutions such as UCSF, University of Louisville, Texas A\&M,
UC Berkeley and others. We do not have good business advisors other than a CFO for hire. Our technical advisory
boards for health, air quality, horticulture/ecology experience. Our current coalition of technical advisors includes Dr.
Aruni Bhatnagar, Chief of the University of Louisville Division of Environmental Medicine and Director of the
Christina Lee Brown Envirome Institute, Dr. Jay Turner, Vice Dean for Education and Professor of Engineering at
Washington University St. Louis, Margaret Gordon of the West Oakland Environmental Indicators Project (WOEIP),
and Rich Baldauf, the roadside vegetated air-barrier expert of US EPA’s office of research and development.

Task 1: Collaboration and Community Engagement
  - Identify an Extreme Heat Working Group that will assist Fresno COG and the consultant in the development of the plan. Fresno COG will invite Caltrans to participate, if desired. The working group will provide input on the analyses, outreach, and proposed projects. Representatives to participate in the working group will be solicited from the City of Fresno, an eastside city, a westside city, County of Fresno, the Fresno County Department of Public Health, an active transportation organization, and a communitybased organization or nonprofit organization.
  - Develop a public outreach plan within the proposed timeline and budget. The consultant will develop a public outreach plan that outlines the format of the outreach activities and identifies specific outreach activities. Such outreach activities should include but not limited to a project website, virtual or/and inperson workshops, popup events, and online interactive tools, etc. Outreach will be conducted in English and Spanish, with material provided in additional languages as needed. The outreach plan will specify how to reach out to the hardtoreach disadvantaged communities, including working with CBOs in the outreach. The work group will provide input for the outreach plan before it is finalized. 
  - Reach out to local agencies to engage them in the planning process and to collect and share data. 
  - Conduct public outreach in line with the Public Outreach Plan developed by Fresno COG, the consultant, and working group. Collaboration and public engagement will be ongoing throughout the project. 
  - Prepare a memo summarizing results of public outreach.
