## From EHCRP
Project Alignment  
COMMON synthesizes the four objectives of the EHCRP, but with a
particular focus on Track A, Goals 2 and 3: developing better assessment
tools, higher resolution modeling, and data collection methods that improve
heathealth observation.
Our Network builds critical datasets of extreme heat into existing
communityrun, paradigmshifting monitoring networks, including IVAN and
SJV Air, the largest communitymanaged remote sensor network in the
country. By placing the best available science and technology into the
hands of the region’s most culturally competent community leaders,
COMMON’s objectives are actionable from day 1 of our timeline. Our
monitoring and outreach partners are grassroots environmental justice
organizations fighting for decades on behalf of voiceless and marginalized
people. The very language of this application prompt, centering the agency
of the local CBOs themselves over that of outside actors, demonstrates
the success of our coalition’s multidecade advocacy for equitycentered
processes in governmentissued grants.
We are proud that our community efforts have successfully set a precedent
for the State to follow. The AB 617 framework grew out of the ability of our
coalition actors to harness lowcost air quality sensors in service of
community needs. The Transforming Climate Communities and California
Climate Investments funding streams, as well as the governmentto
government and Community Air grants, from US EPA and CARB
respectively, also resulted from this communitydriven process. Our
platform adds geographicallyspecific early heat warning systems to these
air quality programs, underscoring heat’s economic impact while empirically
justifying the need for statewide syndromic surveillance in the State’s
hospital system.
**Community Needs and Priorities:**
Though each of the subcontracted CEJC partners fights its own unique
pollution challenges, we are all united in the fight against extreme heat. In
order to adequately assess the vulnerability of our respective communities,
however, we require data that has enough granularity to a) pinpoint high
risk areas and b) correlate that vulnerability with health outcomes. With this
hyperlocal data provided by our technical partners, we can then direct
decisionmakers to implement projects, codes, standards, regulations, and
policies that protect our populations from extreme heat.
Based on a combination of remote sensing data, environmental
simulations, and communityplaced heat stress sensors, the community will
use Hyphae’s AdaptOS platform to generate meterscale thermal stress
metrics. These calculations first integrate 70 meter regional ECOSTRESS
satellite surface temperature data, weather data, and GoogleStreetview
derived skyview/shadefactor imagery for each region. Based on this heat
data, as well as on an assessment of sensitive receptors and mitigation
potential, CEJC’s collaborative process produces a firstlevel prioritization
of target neighborhoods. HDL will then generate a digital twin from LIDAR
data at the 1 meter scale, which will enable hyperlocal environmental
simulations of heat stress in the target neighborhoods. These metrics are
generated iteratively as the community discusses prioritization and
mitigation strategies to adaptively optimize the priority locations based on
heat stress potential (temperature, population, sensitive receptors),
mitigation potential (suitability for addition of cooling infrastructure or route
planning), and logistics (mitigation costs). Community members will place
thermal stress sensors in the target neighborhoods to validate the
simulations. A cohort of volunteers will also be outfitted with mobile sensors
to track thermal stress throughout the activity spaces of the community.
Trusted community leaders then circulate through the chosen
neighborhoods to confirm that the gathering places of its most vulnerable
inhabitants are carefully mapped out and included in the models.
**CommunityInformed Project Design:**
COMMON centers the agency and needs of Central and Imperial valley
communities, many of whom are outdoor workers lacking tree cover and
public shade, and who suffer from poverty, historic trauma, high power
costs, and lack of transportation.
Project Design
COMMON sustains Partner legacies of communitydriven design by
deploying sensor networks, an early warning mobile app, and interventions
designed to mitigate extreme heat. Sensor networks link realtime data to
the mobile app, thus leveraging HDL’s existing environmental simulation
technologies (funded by the National Science Foundation) to alert residents
about heat events and the safest, coolest routes to their destinations.
Personal Heat Sensors are given to workers and heat mitigation projects
(including but not limited to greening) are designed by the community based
on datainformed community feedback. Sensor data and simulations are
then used to validate the efficacy of these mitigation projects.
Community Input
1\. Design begins with community meetings and various outreach
strategies, with a focus on community mapping to gather firsthand accounts
of heat impact and potential solutions.
2\. Continuous feedback gathered from community and data refinement
determines sensor location, app function, and selection of adaptation
areas.
3\. Partner groups are trained to manage sensor network and develop
Capacity.
**How are individuals in heat vulnerable communities represented and given power in the decisionmaking processes of the grant?**
In COMMON’s design and execution, individuals from heatvulnerable
communities are not only represented but are in control of the grant and
central to decisionmaking process. This project leverages existing, long
established EJ community groups to further empower their communities
through heat information. These community groups are directly funded to
lead the direction of the grant. Through the coalition structure. These
community groups create structure and assume the leadership roles in both
planning and implementation phases of the grant. COMMON partners have
already been conducting extensive climate mitigation community outreach,
including town hall meetings, polls and surveys, focus groups, flyering,
doortodoor canvassing, social media posts, and email blasts to ensure
that the project's priorities align with the actual needs of the local residents
through TCC and other grants. These long term, real engagements allow for
our coalition to identify key heatvulnerable areas and this critical data to
the dialogue and have the community inform the placement and types of
interventions, such as sensor locations, cooling centers and heat
mitigation strategies like greening projects. Community members are
trained to install, manage, and interpret data from the heat sensors,
fostering not only involvement but also ownership and skill development.
From Marin\_CARB 5
Hyphae has been in existence for 12 years, has a staff of ten, and has capacity and experience to manage the technical and project management aspects of this project. Hyphae’s interdisciplinary team is made up of biologists, environmental engineers, geospatial data analysts, landscape architects and urban planners that apply multidisciplinary systemsthinking to challenging problems. Hyphae has designed landscapes and managed planting for many projects, including large scale projects, over the last ten years; has experience working with large, complex teams of academic health researchers in partnership with community groups, agencies, urban designers, ecologists, and engineers; and has developed and managed teams with some of the nation's leading medical and environmental health exposure researchers and have received funding from the National Institute of Health (NIH), the Federal Highway Administration (FHWA) and National Science Foundation (NSF) programs to advance the design, engineering, modelling and monitoring of near road green infrastructure. As a missiondriven firm, Hyphae is committed to and at the frontier of researching, designing, modeling, advocating for and delivering healthdriven urban design projects.
Urban Biofilter Past Projects (Hyphae involved): 
  - Greenheart (Louisville, KY): A rigorous longitudinal study on the impact of urban greening on cardiovascular health that involves extensive planting along roadways and in residents’ yards; funded through a multiyear grant from the National Institute of Health
  - Green for Good (Louisville, KY): A completed vegetated barrier pilot at a Louisville elementary school that found significant reduction in exposure to pollution and student blood inflammatory marks after a densely vegetated barrier was planted. Funded by the Funders’ Network for Smart Growth and Livable Communities, Owlsey Brown II Family Foundation
  - Adapt Oakland (West Oakland): An extensive data collection, analysis, community engagement, and development of a neighborhood wide greening plan for West Oakland. Funded by the Strategic Growth Council. 
  - Prescott Greening Feasibility (West Oakland): the initial feasibility and modeling phase of this project, completed in partnership with the Cornell Engineering Zhang Lab. Funded by the Strategic Growth Council
**Task 1: Project Goals and Milestones**
The project seeks to enhance urban air monitoring, modeling, and management through a multifaceted approach combining advanced sensor data processing, modeling at different scales, and community engagement. Community engagement is integral to the success of this project as a whole and will occur throughout these project sections. 
Quantitative Assessment 
This process enables continuous refinement, improving data accuracy and model reliability with each iteration. By integrating feedback and new data, the project adapts to realworld conditions and community needs, creating a responsive framework for air quality management. To meet quality objectives, all CFD models will undergo rigorous verification and validation (V\&V). Verification ensures the model is correctly built, while validation confirms it produces accurate, realistic results. Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) will also be applied to assess and quantify input uncertainties, enhancing confidence in model predictions.
Qualitative Assessment 
**Community Feedback and Engagement: **We will gather community input through surveys and interactive workshops, allowing residents to share their perceptions of air quality, health concerns, and satisfaction with monitoring efforts. Additionally, we will collaborate with communitybased organizations (CBOs) and local health centers to gain insights into community awareness, trust levels, and areas needing further support or communication.
**Stakeholder Engagement and Policymaker Input:** Consultations with CARB and local air districts will ensure project findings align with regulatory priorities, including community health impacts and policy recommendations. We will also track any resulting policy adjustments, funding allocations, or regulatory actions taken by decisionmakers, evaluating their contributions toward achieving environmental justice objectives.
**Task 3: Workforce Development **
The CARB 5 grant will allow MCCR to pay its own people to continue their important work while also training a local community member, Zion, as a monitoring technician and community liaison. Zion is studying at the College of Marin, and represents a valuable opportunity for MCCR to invest in longterm, communityrooted environmental expertise. His role will combine handson technical responsibilities with community outreach, creating a bridge between MCCR’s technical goals and Marin City’s residents. 
Through this grant, Zion will gain comprehensive training in air quality monitoring and data management, learning how to deploy sensors, collect data on pollutants—particularly ultrafine particles—and conduct vehicle emission counts, which are critical to the ecosound barrier’s design and evaluation. His role will allow MCCR to maintain continuity in data collection, particularly during offpeak hours, which are essential to capturing accurate pollution baselines. This focused approach ensures that Zion will have the technical acumen to operate sophisticated air monitoring equipment and conduct meaningful data analysis.
In addition to his technical work, Zion will serve as a community liaison, sharing project updates, gathering feedback from residents, and ensuring that the community’s insights are reflected in the project’s progress. His dual role supports MCCR’s commitment to fostering environmental justice and builds community trust, as Zion is already wellregarded as both a role model and a local advocate. This grant therefore aligns MCCR’s immediate project needs with a sustainable workforce development goal, providing Zion with a foundation to advance his environmental science career and positioning him to lead ongoing air quality efforts in Marin City beyond the scope of this project.
A major component is training the MCCR staff and AQ Technicians and community members on an evidence based design approach and workflow, which includes both air quality monitoring and data assessment. This handson training will help residents understand air quality issues that directly affect their lives, enabling them to take active roles in mitigation pollution through green interventions.
Educational sessions will also incorporate the** **trainthe trainer approach to foster environmental monitoring skills. This model builds a network of local leaders who can share their knowledge within the community, creating a foundation for longterm environmental advocacy and awareness. 
1.  **Coordination and Advocacy with Caltrans and Key Stakeholders  
    **Caltrans’ current plans to elevate the highway and improve stormwater drainage infrastructure create a crucial moment for MCCR to advocate for the community’s needs. By presenting welldocumented designs and predictive models of pollution mitigation, MCCR will leverage this project to push for the ecosound barrier’s integration into Caltrans’ plans. MCCR’s strategic engagement with stakeholders like the Army Corps and Marin Community Foundation reinforces this communityled intervention, demonstrating Marin City’s proactive role in advocating for pollution reduction and infrastructure improvements.
2.  **Laying the Foundation for LongTerm Health and Emissions Reduction  
    **This project not only addresses Marin City’s immediate needs but also establishes a framework for future air quality monitoring and pollution control interventions. The ecosound barrier serves as a foundational case study for communitydriven pollution mitigation, positioning MCCR for ongoing advocacy efforts. Through ongoing data collection, public engagement, and workforce development initiatives, Marin City can advance toward securing LCERP designation and ultimately AB 617 status, ensuring that future infrastructure projects meet both regulatory and community standards.
This structured, phased approach strengthens MCCR’s capacity to address Marin City’s environmental challenges headon while building a sustainable air quality monitoring network. By embedding technical expertise, datadriven design, and community leadership, this project positions Marin City as a model for effective, equitable environmental health initiatives.
**Policy Influence and Regulatory Impact:** Success in policy influence will be measured by the project’s impact on policy and regulatory actions. Project data and findings will aim to support local or regional policy adjustments, expanded monitoring, and environmental health interventions. We will track the number of regulatory or policy changes informed by project findings and document any shifts in resource allocation, followup studies, or new monitoring initiatives prompted by the project’s outcomes.
**Interface with Caltrans and agencies**
The Marin City team faces significant challenges in coordinating with Caltrans, particularly regarding infrastructure projects that impact the community’s flood risk and environmental health. Caltrans’ current highway elevation and repaving plans could intensify flood issues by channeling stormwater directly into Marin City, rather than diverting it into nearby areas like Sausalito, which have more established flood management systems. While Caltrans has held some meetings with Marin City representatives, the team feels that these interactions are largely onesided, with Caltrans presenting plans and gathering feedback but not involving the community in genuine collaborative planning. There is a perceived bias toward prioritizing commuter traffic flow over the environmental justice needs of Marin City residents, heightening the team’s sense of urgency to advocate for more inclusive infrastructure solutions.
To address these issues, the team proposes a multifaceted strategy that includes formal documentation and proactive engagement with Caltrans. This strategy involves consistently documenting meetings and followups to create a written record of the community’s requests and concerns, which can serve as a basis for legal recourse if Caltrans fails to act on them. Additionally, the team plans to develop communitydriven design alternatives, including proposals for an ecobarrier or sound wall, to provide clear visualizations of how community needs can be met. By creating detailed models and drawings that highlight the impact of Caltrans’ plans on Marin City, the team hopes to advocate more effectively for infrastructure adjustments that protect the community from flooding and air pollution. They are also pursuing additional grant funding to support technical advisors and documentation specialists, aiming to bring in external expertise and maintain a consistent advocacy presence in Caltrans meetings.
**Securing Funding for Sustained Engagement**
In addition to CARB funding, the project will seek additional grants focused on stormwater management, environmental justice, and community resilience to enhance project stability. This funding will support dedicated roles for technical advisors and documentation specialists, ensuring that community representatives are wellprepared for ongoing engagement with Caltrans and other agencies. Furthermore, the project will offer workforce development opportunities for local youth and community members, with tailored training to build relevant skills in technical monitoring and facilitation.
From AirSense proposal section on Community Engagement (EPA AQ grant w/ Andres)
**e. Community Engagement Plan Introduction **
AB 617, California’s landmark environmental justice bill of 2017, mandated the implementation of communityled air quality monitoring and emissions reduction programs, placing environmental data and scientific tools into the hands of community organizers that had formerly been handled exclusively by experts. Long before AB 617, however, Hyphae Design Laboratory, founded by Brent Bucknum in 2008, had been practicing this collaborative, communitydriven, evidencebased form of environmental justice advocacy. Hyphae has provided technical assistance to hundreds of local communitybased organizations in collecting data, implementing environmental design solutions, and advocating for their communities before state regulatory agencies. For almost fifteen years, from West Oakland to Stockton, Los Angeles, and the country at large, their partners have fought to address unfair pollution burdens faced by lowincome communities and communities of color. By coordinating activists, technical assistants, academic researchers, air districts, city governments, and federal agencies, they have transformed their environments and achieved significant policy changes in pollution reduction, green infrastructure, and climate resilience. Using hyperlocal mapping and measurement tools developed with funding from the National Science Foundation and the National Institute of Health, Hyphae helps CBOs identify air, soil, and water contamination in their communities, as well as other risks to human health such as extreme heat. These groups then deploy Hyphae’s softwarebased adaptOS platform to simulate, design, and implement environmental mitigation solutions in response. Such interventions range from urban greening to truckrerouting to the design of software applications protecting sensitive receptors during extreme heat events. A persistent weakness hampers the optimal implementation of their combined environmental justice vision, however: the lack of reliable modeling tools. Although lowcoast commercial air sensors can now be reliably calibrated by colocating them with reference monitors, estimating the state of a dynamic system from a series of noisy measurements using techniques like Kalman filtering still lags. Furthermore, the integration of Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) tools for reliable urban airflow modeling and simulation is not yet fully developed, hindering the accurate prediction and analysis of air quality in urban environments. University of South Florida (USF), in collaboration with its contracted partner Hyphae, submits this proposal to the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) to address these critical methodological gaps. Hyphae’s methods, combining outreach with technical expertise, have already secured for CBOs an empirical foundation from which to advocate for fair regulations, standards, and codes. But without adequate urban air quality modeling and estimation tools, communities and air districts cannot effectively assess the costs and benefits of given interventions. That weakness slows down environmental remediation at all stages of the process, and casts doubt on our collective capacity to resolve our present climate crisis. By redressing this computational liability, the EPA stands to expand California’s participatory environmental justice approach to a nationwide scale, thus extending the State’s legacy of leading the nation in the formation of ecological policy. Our partnership structure in this proposed project unites Hyphae’s oldest partners, the West Oakland Environmental Indicators Project (WOEIP) with newer collaborators in Little Manila Rising (Stockton, California) and Climate Resolve (Boyle Heights, Los Angeles). All three organizations actively monitor air quality with inhouse sensor networks, united in their goal to effect lasting policy changes through their local air districts and the California Air Resources Board (CARB). But only WOEIP has so far successfully pressured local transportation authorities to make environmental concessions. We thus recommend applying WOEIP’s successful strategy in West Oakland to South Stockton and Boyle Heights. The EPA can rely on the Hyphae coalition’s proven track record of addressing unfair pollution burdens to achieve regulatory goals. Should our collaboration prove successful, it could serve as a case study for CBOs across the nation in this new chapter of environmental justice. Hyphae’s partnership with WOEIP has grown to include an ensemble of community actors ranging from treeplanting organizations, youth education groups, neighborhood associations, climate resilience activists, local churches, and schools. Together with regulatory agencies, air districts, city governments, and CARB, this Hyphaeassisted coalition is now implementing a multiyear, multimilliondollar urban planning endeavor that combines vegetated barriers, road dieting, and truck rerouting in the Prescott neighborhood of West Oakland. In addition to the Prescott project, Hyphae and WOEIP are also collaborating on West Oakland’s Sustainable Transportation Equity Project (STEP), another CARB initiative aimed at protecting residents from pollution by reducing transportation emissions. After years of vigorous evidencebased advocacy from environmental justice groups, the Oakland Department of Transportation (OakDOT) and the Oakland Parks and Recreation Foundation (OPRF) finally ceded to community demands and agreed to participate in the project’s urban planning. Underlying both projects is a conception of West Oakland as a comprehensive ecosystem linking communitybased organizations with academic researchers, state and federal agencies, and the private sector. Beyond CARB, the coalition’s most immediate regulatory partner is the Bay Area Air Quality Management District (BAAQMD), the entity tasked with enforcing the Bay Area’s portion of AB 617’s mandate. Having served on the region’s Technical Advisory Group since 2017, Brent Bucknum has worked tirelessly to shape its policy agenda while curating its research goals and slate of funding proposals. Since 2017, in quarterly WOEIPled meetings convened in the context of the Prescott grant, Hyphae modeling expert Ivan Heitmann has been presenting Hyphae’s pollution measurement analyses and remediation simulations to a combined audience of community organizations and air district officials. Highranking air district allies include Stephen Reid, Senior Advanced Projects Advisor of the Assessment Inventory and Modeling Department, Song Bai, of the Assessment Inventory and Modeling department, and David Holstius, Ph.D., Senior Advanced Project Advisor in the Planning and Climate Protection department. Over time, these figures have been progressively integrating Hyphae’s models with those of the air district. Meanwhile, the CBOs directed the next phase of the pressure campaign to force the Department of Transportation and CalTrans to take seriously the pollution risks posed to residents by the Port of Oakland and adjacent freeways. The West Oakland coalition was finally vindicated with the 2019 passing of the West Oakland Community Air Action Plan (WOCAP) which compelled CalTrans, the Department of Transportation, the Port of Oakland, and local refineries to make substantial regulatory concessions. These included stricter emissions controls on trucks and other heavyduty vehicles, as well as a mandate to enhance air quality monitoring and invest in green infrastructure to mitigate pollution impacts. Furthermore, the WOCAP obliged future transportationrelated projects to incorporate community feedback into their planning processes. The Plan advanced a list of eightynine proposed pollutionmitigating strategies, ranging from urban greening to truck rerouting, optimized building envelopes, and clean electrification. But due to persistent skepticism among many scientists regarding the accuracy of modeling, the steering committee still lacks an empirical basis by which to effectively simulate which of these proposed solutions would have maximum impact and highest benefittocost ratio. More effective modeling could effectively demonstrate at a hyperlocal level exactly how tree canopy, vegetation, traffic, and shade could be optimally configured to protect environmental health. In this grant application, we propose to use WOEIP’s successful pressure 
### Task 2: Review Existing Plans, Guidelines, and Policies
Review existing federal, state, and local plans, policies and guidelines regarding climate adaptation related to extreme heat.
Deliverable: Summary of climate adaptation plans, policies, and guidelines related to extreme heat

~~**Extreme Heat Conditions and Vulnerability:**~~
~~If greenhouse gas emissions continue to rise at current rates, the~~
~~temperature in downtown Fresno will hit 106.6 fahrenheit 43 times a year.~~
~~Classified as Heat Health Events (HHEs), studies have predicted that~~
~~these spikes in heat could last up to two weeks long in our region (Thorne~~
~~et al., 2018). Adapting to these conditions and durations will require large~~
~~scale targeted neighborhood community interventions.Though the risk to~~
~~urban environments is well-known, COMMON also seeks to draw attention~~
~~to the less widely understood Rural Heat Island Effect (RHIE).~~
~~There exists an abundance of federal, state and local public health~~
~~information on extreme heat from fact sheets, websites, and checklists, but~~
~~these sources often provide only general guidelines, with broad county-~~
~~level data. Tools like Caladapt use downscaled daily CMIP5 Climate~~
~~Projections but are also at a resolution of 3 miles, so entire cities are~~
~~sometimes represented as a single data point. Moreover, existing heat~~
~~warning metrics that measure only temperature and humidity fail to~~
~~accurately record critical thermal comfort variables such as wind flow,~~
~~radiant temperature and land use, that significantly drive heat stress.~~
~~These tools also lack critical, subjective, community driven data of how and~~
~~where people live, work, gather and move. Therefore the existing heat~~
~~awareness tools are inadequate to target impacts and generate effective~~
~~solutions. To more accurately warn the public about heat risk, microclimate~~
~~variables need to be integrated into community models forecasting and~~
~~response services also need to be updated to take into account diverse~~
~~populations. Community driven population data can also help improve~~
~~healthcare response to target the ripple effect, through which extreme heat~~
~~renders the public more vulnerable to mental health emergencies and~~
~~chronic health conditions from migraine and rosacea, to lupus and~~
~~rheumatoid arthritis, cardiovascular disease, respiratory distress, and~~
~~kidney failure.~~

~~**Interface with Caltrans and agencies**~~
~~The Marin City team faces significant challenges in coordinating with Caltrans, particularly regarding infrastructure projects that impact the community’s flood risk and environmental health. Caltrans’ current highway elevation and repaving plans could intensify flood issues by channeling stormwater directly into Marin City, rather than diverting it into nearby areas like Sausalito, which have more established flood management systems. While Caltrans has held some meetings with Marin City representatives, the team feels that these interactions are largely one-sided, with Caltrans presenting plans and gathering feedback but not involving the community in genuine collaborative planning. There is a perceived bias toward prioritizing commuter traffic flow over the environmental justice needs of Marin City residents, heightening the team’s sense of urgency to advocate for more inclusive infrastructure solutions.~~
~~To address these issues, the team proposes a multi-faceted strategy that includes formal documentation and proactive engagement with Caltrans. This strategy involves consistently documenting meetings and follow-ups to create a written record of the community’s requests and concerns, which can serve as a basis for legal recourse if Caltrans fails to act on them. Additionally, the team plans to develop community-driven design alternatives, including proposals for an eco-barrier or sound wall, to provide clear visualizations of how community needs can be met. By creating detailed models and drawings that highlight the impact of Caltrans’ plans on Marin City, the team hopes to advocate more effectively for infrastructure adjustments that protect the community from flooding and air pollution. They are also pursuing additional grant funding to support technical advisors and documentation specialists, aiming to bring in external expertise and maintain a consistent advocacy presence in Caltrans meetings.~~
~~**Securing Funding for Sustained Engagement**~~

This funding will support dedicated roles for technical advisors and documentation specialists, ensuring that community representatives are well-prepared for ongoing engagement with Caltrans and other agencies. Furthermore, the project will offer workforce development opportunities for local youth and community members, with tailored training to build relevant skills in technical monitoring and facilitation.~~
## Task 3: Extreme Heat Vulnerability Analysis
  - “Identify extreme heat vulnerability indicators and develop methodology for the extreme heat vulnerability analysis including feedback from the working group. 
  - Prepare a summary memo of methodology and results, including a list of areas most vulnerable to extreme heat for Fresno COG and working group review. 
Deliverables:
Methodology for Extreme Heat Vulnerability Analysis 
Extreme Heat Vulnerability Analysis Summary Memo
[Harry Hines Evidence-based design methodology report](https://docs.google.com/document/d/1pjgqG3wJG3j3V4w3kpBcz6cwrEsE_ZMVqWDp1iUnlqE/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.mtix9t3ba8qx)
[Green\_Spine\_Design\_Coordination\_Hyphae\_Scope\_and\_Schedule](https://docs.google.com/document/d/1rjLWnWYy-2Vy80-5FDGFUZKCM8VPwrh1TV7bNl2qDh0/edit?tab=t.0)
[DepaveLA Report (internal)](https://docs.google.com/document/d/1wk9SGekva7BLRg-sZzOo_sm15Vw8ZRWuJ8FCkbaVFDM/edit?tab=t.0)
[Depave Outline Full  ](https://docs.google.com/document/d/1fnKqrOIHnoRx_dHY2V88cYc7r1OY4EAwM3xhG3UUn6M/edit?tab=t.0)
### ~~**VERSION 2**~~
~~The Heat Vulnerability Assessment employs a highly detailed and communityfocused approach to understand and address the complexities of extreme heat exposure. Traditional heat mapping tools often lack the resolution necessary for actionable, communitylevel interventions, limiting their utility in rural and mixeduse environments like Fresno County. To overcome these limitations, our analysis integrates advanced data sources and modeling techniques that offer unprecedented precision in identifying heatvulnerable areas and populations. By synthesizing meteorological land use classifications with innovative population mapping systems, the assessment provides a detailed understanding of how heat is distributed and experienced across diverse local contexts.~~
~~This advanced methodology goes beyond static heat mapping by incorporating realworld variables such as wind flow, humidity, and radiant temperature into neighborhoodspecific simulations. These models are further enhanced through continuous engagement with community stakeholders, ensuring the integration of hyperlocal knowledge about demographic patterns and daily behaviors. This dual emphasis on technical rigor and community relevance allows us to deliver practical, targeted solutions that are actionable and tailored to the unique challenges of Fresno County. Together, these tools and insights form the backbone of an equitable and impactful heat mitigation strategy.~~

  - ~~Addresses limitations of traditional data like EcoStress and CalAdapt that lack communitylevel resolution. ~~

  - ~~Population Data Integration:~~

  - ~~NeighborhoodScale Simulation Models for Urban Heat Island (UHI) as well as Rural Heat Island Effects (RHIE) effects: ~~

This comprehensive approach will result in an evidencebased, communityinformed plan that provides Fresno County with practical strategies for reducing extreme heat exposure in its most vulnerable communities.

~~Paragraph form:~~
1.  ~~**Paragraph form**~~~~:~~
~~The project's technical approach represents a significant advancement beyond conventional heat mapping methods, addressing the fundamental limitation of traditional data sources like EcoStress and CalAdapt that lack sufficient resolution for communitylevel action. The approach integrates multiple sophisticated data sources, beginning with Local Climate Zone (LCZ) classification that provides detailed meteorological land use categories spanning farmland, city areas, and varying densities of subdivisions. This classification is enhanced by two innovative population mapping systems: Harvard's Popgrid, which utilizes Microsoft's building data layer to distribute population estimates at the building level rather than census tract, and RTI's synthetic populations system, which applies mathematical approaches to distribute demographic data with unprecedented precision.~~
~~The heart of the technical approach lies in its sophisticated modeling methodology. The team builds detailed simulation models for each neighborhood, incorporating wind data to calculate thermal comfort metrics. The Universal Thermal Climate Index measurements integrate air temperature, radiant temperature, humidity, and wind data to create a comprehensive understanding of how temperature is actually experienced by residents. This approach achieves square meterlevel resolution, producing something akin to wet bulb temperature calculations but at a much more granular level than previously possible. These models are specifically calibrated for and run in each focus community.~~
~~What makes this approach particularly powerful is its practical applicability. The modeling can identify temperature variations of 2030 degrees within distances as short as 2030 feet, helping communities identify immediate solutions even before interventions are implemented. Unlike traditional heat mapping that might simply show an area as a "red spot," this approach can pinpoint specific cool areas, such as those near creeks or under tree canopy. This is especially effective in the Central Valley's dry climate. The models are validated through strategically placed sensors that measure temperature, humidity, and globe temperature, creating wet bulb globe temperature equivalents at the meter level.~~
~~Perhaps most importantly, the technical approach is deeply integrated with community knowledge and needs. Models are developed in continuous dialogue with communities, incorporating hyperlocal demographic and behavioral input  such as identifying frequently used bus stops or gathering spaces. This integration helps prioritize intervention locations and provides immediately actionable information for community members. The approach effectively avoids the paralysis often induced by traditional "doom and gloom" heat mapping by identifying specific, achievable improvements. Rather than presenting communities with overwhelming, tractlevel heat data, it provides meterlevel resolution that's particularly wellsuited for rural and mixeduse communities. This combination of technical sophistication and community integration creates a powerful tool for understanding and addressing urban heat islands at a truly local level.~~
## Task 4: Conduct Pedestrian and Bicyclist Traffic Analysis and Location Analysis  
  - Develop methodology for pedestrian and bicyclist traffic analysis and location analysis. 
  - Conduct location analysis to identify preliminary vulnerable corridors using results from the extreme heat vulnerability analysis and pedestrian and bicyclist traffic analysis. 
  - Prepare a summary memo of analyses results for review from FCOG and working group. 
Deliverables: Pedestrian and Bicyclist Traffic Analysis and Location Analysis Summary Memo

## From Green Latinos draft 
NARRATIVE:
Hyphae Design Laboratory approaches urban forestry and green infrastructure through the lens of environmental justice and community empowerment. While conventional urban forest master plans provide valuable highlevel guidance, we specialize in bridging the critical gap between broad planning documents and actual community implementation. Our experience has shown that the greatest challenges often lie not in the technical planning, but in translating these plans into actionable, communityled initiatives that create lasting impact.
Our approach fundamentally reimagines how tree planting and green infrastructure projects are implemented in environmental justice communities. Rather than following the standard model of filling individual tree wells—which requires extensive outreach for minimal return and often stretches community capacity thin—we identify strategic, highimpact opportunities that maximize environmental benefits while minimizing administrative burden. By focusing on locations such as freeway corridors, school zones, and public parks, we can achieve significant tree canopy expansion through fewer administrative touchpoints, allowing communities to plant hundreds of trees through agreements with just a few key stakeholders rather than seeking approval from hundreds of individual property owners.
Central to our methodology is a commitment to building local economic capacity alongside environmental improvements. Instead of centralizing all project management and implementation within nonprofit organizations, we help communities identify and engage local, minorityowned landscaping businesses to perform the work. This approach not only ensures more efficient project execution but also keeps resources within the community, creating a sustainable model where local businesses become longterm partners in maintaining and expanding urban forestry initiatives. When community members see their neighbors and local businesses leading implementation, it strengthens buyin and ensures more effective longterm stewardship.
Our work extends beyond traditional urban forestry to address the diverse environmental justice challenges facing communities. Drawing from our extensive experience with farmworker communities, warehouse districts, and industrial zones, we understand that effective green infrastructure must be tailored to specific community contexts. This might mean designing vegetative barriers for air quality near warehouses, creating protective greenery near pesticideimpacted areas, or developing innovative solutions for extreme heat mitigation. Our tools and approaches are built from the ground up to address these varied challenges, ensuring that each project not only increases green space but also meaningfully addresses critical environmental health concerns.
Through years of handson experience in communities from Oakland to Los Angeles, we've developed a comprehensive strategy that emphasizes coalition building, maintenance planning, and longterm stewardship alongside initial implementation. By focusing on strategic locations, building local capacity, and maintaining a steadfast commitment to environmental justice principles, we help communities transform highlevel plans into tangible environmental improvements that build both ecological and economic resilience.
**From Green Latinos**
**Expertise in Urban Forestry Activities**
Hyphae Design Lab has a strong track record in urban forestry, leveraging expertise in tree inventories, canopy assessments, site preparation, tree planting, and maintenance. We approach each project with a comprehensive understanding of the technical, environmental, and community aspects of urban greening.
**1. Tree Inventory and Canopy Assessment**
  - **Tree Inventory:** We use advanced tools like **TreePlotter** and **ArcGIS Field Maps** to conduct tree inventories, tracking species, health, location, and age of existing trees in urban settings.
  - **Canopy Assessment:** By utilizing **LiDAR** data, including **Leaf Area Index (LAI)** and **Leaf Area Density (LAD)**, we assess canopy cover, which helps prioritize planting areas and measure potential environmental benefits like air quality improvement and temperature reduction.
**2. Site Preparation**
  - **Site Evaluation:** We assess urban sites for tree planting suitability, reviewing soil quality, available space, infrastructure, and potential challenges such as existing utilities. This is aligned with city ordinances (e.g., 811 guidelines) and environmental regulations.
  - **Preparation:** We manage soil preparation, including excavation for **tree wells**, and ensure compliance with city ordinances to ensure longterm tree health. Our team coordinates with municipal agencies to ensure the tree planting follows all regulations.
**3. Tree Planting and Maintenance**
  - **Tree Planting:** Our ISAcertified arborists are trained in proper tree planting techniques, accommodating various types of nursery stock including **ball and burlap**, **container**, and **bare root** trees. We conduct **planting demonstrations** and emphasize the importance of watering, mulching, and pruning.
  - **Maintenance and Stewardship:** We create customized **maintenance plans** for tree stewardship, which outline how trees will be cared for in the long term. These plans are designed to empower community members and organizations to take on stewardship responsibilities.
**4. Workforce Development and Community Engagement**
  - **Youth Training Programs:** We have created **paid youth tree programs** targeting high school and college students from local communities. Youth participants receive training in **tree planting, watering, pruning, species identification**, and **tree health assessments**. Additionally, they learn about orchard care, community outreach, and the importance of urban trees.
  - **Adult Leader Programs:** We provide **paid stipends** for adult leaders who assist with community engagement, organizing **tree planting events**, **giveaways**, and **greening projects**. These leaders also liaise with city officials, ensuring that community needs are represented.
  - **Community Events:** Hyphae has hosted **biannual tree planting events** and **fruit tree giveaways**, offering an opportunity for local residents to get involved and learn about urban forestry.
Community Engagement Strategies for Urban Forestry
1\. **Outreach Strategies**
  - **Community Surveys and Social Media:** We assess the preferred communication methods in each community (surveys, social media, radio, or community events) and tailor our outreach accordingly.
  - **Feedback Loops:** We implement systems like **suggestion boxes** or online platforms for ongoing community feedback, ensuring that the community’s voice is heard throughout the project. This is a valuable tool to gauge satisfaction and identify areas for improvement.
**2. Program Assessment and Goal Setting**
  - **Review Past Programs:** By evaluating previous tree programs, we determine what worked well and where there were challenges. This helps in setting realistic goals for new tree planting initiatives.
  - **Collaborative Goal Setting:** Community input is integral in shaping program goals, ensuring they reflect the local needs, such as increasing canopy cover, improving air quality, or fostering workforce development.
**3. Community Leadership Development**
  - **Stipended Leaders:** We work to develop a group of **community leaders** who can advocate for the project, organize events, and engage neighbors. This model encourages communitydriven stewardship.
  - **Mapping and Citizen Science:** By combining **community mapping projects** with citizen science efforts, we help communities identify priority planting locations, such as schools, bus stops, and gathering areas.
In collaboration with **GreenLatinos' Justicia Y Equidad Grants Manager**, we ensure transparent and efficient budget management.

The strategies should consider active transportation and public transit and should have cobenefits to public health, natural ecosystems, social equity, the economy, and greenhouse gas emissions.

**Merissa MacDonald, ISACertified Arborist and Urban Forestry Specialist, Project Manager  PD3010A**
**Current Position**: Arborist and Partnerships Manager, Hyphae Design Lab
  
Merissa MacDonald is a certified arborist with deep experience in urban forestry, coalitionbuilding, and communityled green infrastructure projects. Her previous role as Tree Program Coordinator at Bartram’s Garden in Philadelphia showcases her success in fostering environmental equity and community engagement in Southwest Philadelphia. Merissa continues to support this program as a consultant, providing her expertise and local insight as a former neighbor.
At Bartram’s Garden, Merissa created and led the Southwest Tree Tenders group, securing funding from the Knight Foundation and the Pennsylvania Horticultural Society (PHS) to plant over 400 trees in underserved neighborhoods. She developed an internship program, the Southwest Tree Crew, which trained and employed up to 16 local high school students each year. Through this, Merissa empowered youth to become certified PHS Tree Tenders and advocates for environmental justice, training them in arboricultural practices, public speaking, and workforce development skills.
Merissa's leadership extended to founding the Southwest Tree Coalition, a network that unites community members, artists, gardeners, and landscape architects to enhance tree planting efforts. Under her direction, the coalition built a strong community presence through social media, branding, and highprofile planting events, including the annual SpringFest event, which attracted over 2,000 participants.
**Specific Responsibilities and Role within Hyphae Design Lab**:
  - **Partnerships Management**: Cultivates and manages relationships with key stakeholders, ranging from longstanding collaborators to new alliances, to strengthen community forestry and workforce development initiatives.

  - **Community Engagement and Outreach**: Leads outreach and communications efforts to promote program goals, sharing resources and insights through events, presentations, and partnerships.
  - **Workforce Development**: Designs training programs and resources to upskill individuals in urban forestry practices, with a focus on engaging underserved and bilingual communities.
Merissa’s background includes extensive work with lowcanopy, environmentally impacted areas, especially with underserved and bilingual (African Frenchspeaking) communities. Her experience leading inclusive, communitybased environmental programs has fostered strong connections and lasting impact in Southwest Philadelphia. Through her role at Hyphae, Merissa continues to advocate for communityled, equitable green infrastructure solutions tailored to address the unique needs of disadvantaged communities.
**Juan Peñaloza Gutierrez, PhD  
Role**: CFD Specialist  
**Position**: Software Engineer (CFD Modeler), Hyphae Design Laboratory
**  
**Dr. Juan Peñaloza Gutierrez brings advanced expertise in Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) to his role as a Software Engineer at Hyphae Design Lab. With a PhD in Mechanical Engineering from the University of South Florida, Juan specializes in turbulence modeling, pollution dispersion, and air quality simulations in complex urban environments. His research includes advanced simulations, such as ReynoldsAveraged NavierStokes (RANS) and LargeEddy Simulations (LES), applied to pollution transport, windfield analysis, and aerosol dispersion in spaces like hospital isolation rooms. Juan has presented his work at leading conferences, including the Ocean Sciences Meeting and the Annual APS Division of Fluid Dynamics Meeting.
At Hyphae Design Lab, Juan’s primary role involves using CFD to model air pollution dispersion and assess the effectiveness of green infrastructure in mitigating air quality issues. His responsibilities include:
  - **CFD Model Development and Application**: Developing highresolution RANS and LES models to simulate pollutant dispersion around urban green infrastructure and examining the influence of natural barriers on air quality.
  - **Project Analysis and Reporting**: Providing datadriven insights to support environmental designs and interventions, refining models to address projectspecific environmental challenges.
  - **Technical Consultation**: Advising teams on the integration of CFD results with ecological and urban design strategies, ensuring project goals align with local environmental needs.
**  
**As a bilingual engineer, Juan offers accessibility in Spanishspeaking communities, helping bridge technical language barriers in underserved areas. His work at Hyphae Design Lab often involves collaborating with local stakeholders to make complex CFD insights understandable for residents and community groups, particularly those in areas affected by industrial pollution. This approach allows him to tailor his technical expertise in ways that promote inclusive and effective solutions for air quality improvement in underserved urban neighborhoods.
**Mei Visco, Ecological Designer and Project Manager, Hyphae Design Lab**
Mei Visco is an ecological designer and experienced project manager with a focus on urban forestry, air quality improvement, and communityled greening initiatives. Mei has managed several highimpact projects funded by agencies like CARB, SEP, MTC, and CNRA, partnering closely with local communities and city organizations to address environmental justice and promote sustainable urban design.

The West Oakland community, historically underserved, benefits from a longterm stewardship plan Mei developed by engaging local treeplanting groups for resource sharing and maintenance.
