## Material from EHCRP Proposal
### Project Vision
The vision of the California Environmental Justice Coalition is to form a
communityled research observatory and task force capable of monitoring
and mitigating the deadly effects of extreme heat in the Central and
Imperial valleys. Our project, the Communityled Microclimate Observation
Network (COMMON), aims to transform these regions into models of
climate resilience, and brings a unique focus on farmworkerpopulated
agricultural areas that are often left out of extreme heat remediation efforts
directed towards cities.
In this endeavor, CEJC has partnered with environmental justice member
organizations with proven records of championing their community’s health
and environment. These organizations, supported by technical partners,
stand to make a historic intervention in the field by correlating hyperlocal
extreme heat data with population and hospital data that will provide
updated heathealth intervention measures, provide better outreach and
early warning systems, and an empirical basis for updating codes,
standards, and regulations that presently fail to protect vulnerable
populations from extreme heat.
As COMMON is designed not only to monitor but also to actively respond
to extreme heat, both with emergency outreach tools and climate
adaptation solutions, its goals of equity, community leadership, and
scientific rigor are in clear alignment with those of the EHCRP.
### Project Description
COMMON aims to enhance the climate resilience of the San Joaquin and
Imperial Valleys with communitydriven solutions. The following 30month
implementation timeline combines communityled outreach, sensor
installation, data collection, software development, and mitigation solutions.
Year 1: Coalitionbuilding and Citizen Science
 CEJC and Partners conduct rigorous community outreach campaigns
across the San Joaquin and Imperial valleys to identify heatvulnerable
neighborhoods.
 Simultaneously, technical Partners from NASA’s Jet Propulsion Lab (JPL),
Hyphae Design Labs (HDL), and UC Merced (UCM) model the area’s
potential highrisk environments. High resolution modeling, usable within
first year of project, provides communities with data to choose where to
deploy sensors, conduct simulations, and plot mitigation strategies. HDL
then trains monitoring technicians of respective Partners on assembly and
installation of open source thermal stress sensors, as well as in use of
Hyphae’s AdaptOS platform to collect and interpret the data.
Year 2 and 3: Immediate Use of Data & Sensor Deployment
 HDL develops an extreme heat early warning and outreach mobile app
capable of alerting residents about heat events and guiding users to
cooling centers and sensitive receptor sites, avoiding hotspots along the
way.
 By Month 24, Partners and HDL have installed sensor network in selected
neighborhoods,
 Partners assist HDL, JPL and UCM in providing communitydriven data
overlay of community’s primary walking and transit routes, gathering
places, and sensitive receptors.
 Hyphae creates high resolution digital twins of geographical areas
prioritized by communities.
 Partners and community finalize targeted greening sites with Tree Fresno.
Greening Projects and Adaptation Reporting
 In year three, installed pilot greening projects are outfitted with sensors to
monitor impact on local microclimates and evaluate their effectiveness as
natural cooling measures.
 Project culminates in report detailing outcomes/efficacy of interventions,
community feedback, and models for expanding COMMON to other regions.
### Project Alignment (w/ California Extreme Heat Action Plan) 
COMMON synthesizes the four objectives of the EHCRP, but with a
particular focus on Track A, Goals 2 and 3: developing better assessment
tools, higher resolution modeling, and data collection methods that improve
heathealth observation.
Our Network builds critical datasets of extreme heat into existing
communityrun, paradigmshifting monitoring networks, including IVAN and
SJV Air, the largest communitymanaged remote sensor network in the
country. By placing the best available science and technology into the
hands of the region’s most culturally competent community leaders,
COMMON’s objectives are actionable from day 1 of our timeline. Our
monitoring and outreach partners are grassroots environmental justice
organizations fighting for decades on behalf of voiceless and marginalized
people. The very language of this application prompt, centering the agency
of the local CBOs themselves over that of outside actors, demonstrates
the success of our coalition’s multidecade advocacy for equitycentered
processes in governmentissued grants.
We are proud that our community efforts have successfully set a precedent
for the State to follow. The AB 617 framework grew out of the ability of our
coalition actors to harness lowcost air quality sensors in service of
community needs. The Transforming Climate Communities and California
Climate Investments funding streams, as well as the governmentto
government and Community Air grants, from US EPA and CARB
respectively, also resulted from this communitydriven process. Our
platform adds geographicallyspecific early heat warning systems to these
air quality programs, underscoring heat’s economic impact while empirically
justifying the need for statewide syndromic surveillance in the State’s
hospital system.
### Community Needs and Priorities:
Though each of the subcontracted CEJC partners fights its own unique
pollution challenges, we are all united in the fight against extreme heat. In
order to adequately assess the vulnerability of our respective communities,
however, we require data that has enough granularity to a) pinpoint high
risk areas and b) correlate that vulnerability with health outcomes. With this
hyperlocal data provided by our technical partners, we can then direct
decisionmakers to implement projects, codes, standards, regulations, and
policies that protect our populations from extreme heat.
Based on a combination of remote sensing data, environmental
simulations, and communityplaced heat stress sensors, the community will
use Hyphae’s AdaptOS platform to generate meterscale thermal stress
metrics. These calculations first integrate 70 meter regional ECOSTRESS
satellite surface temperature data, weather data, and GoogleStreetview
derived skyview/shadefactor imagery for each region. Based on this heat
data, as well as on an assessment of sensitive receptors and mitigation
potential, CEJC’s collaborative process produces a firstlevel prioritization
of target neighborhoods. HDL will then generate a digital twin from LIDAR
data at the 1 meter scale, which will enable hyperlocal environmental
simulations of heat stress in the target neighborhoods. These metrics are
generated iteratively as the community discusses prioritization and
mitigation strategies to adaptively optimize the priority locations based on
heat stress potential (temperature, population, sensitive receptors),
mitigation potential (suitability for addition of cooling infrastructure or route
planning), and logistics (mitigation costs). Community members will place
thermal stress sensors in the target neighborhoods to validate the
simulations. A cohort of volunteers will also be outfitted with mobile sensors
to track thermal stress throughout the activity spaces of the community.
Trusted community leaders then circulate through the chosen
neighborhoods to confirm that the gathering places of its most vulnerable
inhabitants are carefully mapped out and included in the models.
### CommunityInformed Project Design:
COMMON centers the agency and needs of Central and Imperial valley
communities, many of whom are outdoor workers lacking tree cover and
public shade, and who suffer from poverty, historic trauma, high power
costs, and lack of transportation.
Project Design
COMMON sustains Partner legacies of communitydriven design by
deploying sensor networks, an early warning mobile app, and interventions
designed to mitigate extreme heat. Sensor networks link realtime data to
the mobile app, thus leveraging HDL’s existing environmental simulation
technologies (funded by the National Science Foundation) to alert residents
about heat events and the safest, coolest routes to their destinations.
Personal Heat Sensors are given to workers and heat mitigation projects
(including but not limited to greening) are designed by the community based
on datainformed community feedback. Sensor data and simulations are
then used to validate the efficacy of these mitigation projects.
Community Input
1\. Design begins with community meetings and various outreach
strategies, with a focus on community mapping to gather firsthand accounts
of heat impact and potential solutions.
2\. Continuous feedback gathered from community and data refinement
determines sensor location, app function, and selection of adaptation
areas.
3\. Partner groups are trained to manage sensor network and develop
Capacity.
### Extreme Heat Conditions and Vulnerability:
If greenhouse gas emissions continue to rise at current rates, the
temperature in downtown Fresno will hit 106.6 fahrenheit 43 times a year.
Classified as Heat Health Events (HHEs), studies have predicted that
these spikes in heat could last up to two weeks long in our region (Thorne
et al., 2018). Adapting to these conditions and durations will require large
scale targeted neighborhood community interventions.Though the risk to
urban environments is wellknown, COMMON also seeks to draw attention
to the less widely understood Rural Heat Island Effect (RHIE).
There exists an abundance of federal, state and local public health
information on extreme heat from fact sheets, websites, and checklists, but
these sources often provide only general guidelines, with broad county
level data. Tools like Caladapt use downscaled daily CMIP5 Climate
Projections but are also at a resolution of 3 miles, so entire cities are
sometimes represented as a single data point. Moreover, existing heat
warning metrics that measure only temperature and humidity fail to
accurately record critical thermal comfort variables such as wind flow,
radiant temperature and land use, that significantly drive heat stress.
These tools also lack critical, subjective, community driven data of how and
where people live, work, gather and move. Therefore the existing heat
awareness tools are inadequate to target impacts and generate effective
solutions. To more accurately warn the public about heat risk, microclimate
variables need to be integrated into community models forecasting and
response services also need to be updated to take into account diverse
populations. Community driven population data can also help improve
healthcare response to target the ripple effect, through which extreme heat
renders the public more vulnerable to mental health emergencies and
chronic health conditions from migraine and rosacea, to lupus and
rheumatoid arthritis, cardiovascular disease, respiratory distress, and
kidney failure.
### How are individuals in heat vulnerable communities represented and given power in the decisionmaking processes of the grant?
In COMMON’s design and execution, individuals from heatvulnerable
communities are not only represented but are in control of the grant and
central to decisionmaking process. This project leverages existing, long
established EJ community groups to further empower their communities
through heat information. These community groups are directly funded to
lead the direction of the grant. Through the coalition structure. These
community groups create structure and assume the leadership roles in both
planning and implementation phases of the grant. COMMON partners have
already been conducting extensive climate mitigation community outreach,
including town hall meetings, polls and surveys, focus groups, flyering,
doortodoor canvassing, social media posts, and email blasts to ensure
that the project's priorities align with the actual needs of the local residents
through TCC and other grants. These long term, real engagements allow for
our coalition to identify key heatvulnerable areas and this critical data to
the dialogue and have the community inform the placement and types of
interventions, such as sensor locations, cooling centers and heat
mitigation strategies like greening projects. Community members are
trained to install, manage, and interpret data from the heat sensors,
fostering not only involvement but also ownership and skill development.
### Explain barriers to applying with a local and/or regional public entity
The coalition has intentionally proposed a communitybased project which
precludes a public entity as coapplicant. But we do intend to work with
agencies and public landowners in our process.
A common barrier to placing sensor networks and planting trees is in
securing both permits from local governments for public property
placements and buyin from local property owners to place materials on
their property. In our case, however, coalition Partners have secured both
the consent of local property owners and signed agreements with local
governments for tree planting. Partners have already installed air pollution
sensors throughout their respective regions. We can add heat sensors to
those same locations without incurring additional overhead for site
logistics. Meanwhile Tree Fresno’s existing agreements with local
governments allow ongoing treeplanting efforts. To demonstrate this, we
have included letters from Caltrans, the City of Fresno, and schools
showing ongoing support for Tree Fresno work.
### Engagement: How you will engage with local and regional public entities and/or
### tribes throughout the project?
As described above, our choice to propose a communitybased project with
no public entity as coapplicant is first a practical decision. But it is also a
choice rooted in our coalition’s historical trauma as migrant farmworkers
and survivors of redlined communities. Regarding air quality in particular,
many of our members are reluctant to share data with the State, especially
if it could impact their property value.
We eagerly hope to add native tribes into our coalition in future rounds of
funding, however. We have been in talks with Mariko Falke, the Executive
Tribal Liaison of California’s Department of Water Resources (DWR),
about future opportunities for partnership, as well as with Desiree Ramirez,
of DWR’s Financial Assistance Branch (FAB). Though these talks revealed
a shared commitment to address the overlapping issues of heat, drought,
and water rights, they also impressed upon us the value of adopting a long
term trustbuilding approach in forming partnerships with groups that share
their own privacy concerns and justifiable ambivalence towards State
actors. As tribal governments are not topdown institutions, they
meticulously consult the whole community before embarking partnerships.
### Partnership Development
As COMMON evolves, we fully intend to partner with tribes and local public
authorities, as well as government agencies and public landowners. Though
this phase of COMMON’s evolution does not propose naturebased
solutions on public property, we hope that in the future expansion of the
Observation Network, we can secure public cooperation on a number of
large implementation projects installed on public rightofways. These
include installing green corridors along public transportation routes, “green
oases” protecting mobile home parks populated by farmworkers, and “cool
groves” that provide shade in agricultural hotspots.
### Describe how the proposed project will prioritize strategies and outcomes that provide climate change adaptation cobenefits, especially for the benefit of vulnerable communities?
The proposed extreme heat monitoring and mitigation network offers a
multifaceted approach to addressing the challenges posed by climate
change, particularly for vulnerable communities. By generating hyperlocal
predictions of biophysical heat stress and providing a userfriendly app, the
project empowers community members to navigate extreme heat events
safely. The installation of thermal stress sensors in neighborhoods and the
provision of mobile sensors to farmworkers and other atrisk populations,
when combined with health data from UCM will generate valuable data to
better understand the impact of extreme heat on public health outcomes
and healthcare costs. .
This project prioritizes social equity by focusing on environmental justice
communities who are on the frontlines of public health challenges related to
climate change. The data collected will be used to inform public policy,
codes, standards, and regulations, particularly in protecting workers from
extreme heat conditions. By quantifying the economic impact of extreme
heat on the healthcare system, the project will draw attention to the urgent
need for heat mitigation strategies and health disparities caused by
extreme heat's disproportionate impact on marginalized communities.
Furthermore, the project will create jobs in sensor fabrication and LIDAR
driving, supporting the local economy while improving transportation
infrastructure through the development of a routing app that minimizes heat
stress for pedestrians. By associating heat events with hospital
admissions data, it will become possible to code heatrelated illnesses into
hospital admissions procedures, and by combining hospital admissions
data with hyperlocal temperature data, the project will provide a more
accurate picture of the health risks and healthcare costs associated with
extreme heat, informing tailored interventions for various populations,
including athletes, firefighters, and outdoor workers.
Overall, this project offers a comprehensive approach to climate change
adaptation, delivering social equity, public health, economic, and
environmental cobenefits.
## Material from EHCRP Interview Process 
The project's technical approach represents a significant advancement beyond conventional heat mapping methods, addressing the fundamental limitation of traditional data sources like EcoStress and CalAdapt that lack sufficient resolution for communitylevel action. The approach integrates multiple sophisticated data sources, beginning with Local Climate Zone (LCZ) classification that provides detailed meteorological land use categories spanning farmland, city areas, and varying densities of subdivisions. This classification is enhanced by two innovative population mapping systems: Harvard's Popgrid, which utilizes Microsoft's building data layer to distribute population estimates at the building level rather than census tract, and RTI's synthetic populations system, which applies mathematical approaches to distribute demographic data with unprecedented precision.
The heart of the technical approach lies in its sophisticated modeling methodology. The team builds detailed simulation models for each neighborhood, incorporating wind data to calculate thermal comfort metrics. The Universal Thermal Climate Index measurements integrate air temperature, radiant temperature, humidity, and wind data to create a comprehensive understanding of how temperature is actually experienced by residents. This approach achieves square meterlevel resolution, producing something akin to wet bulb temperature calculations but at a much more granular level than previously possible. These models are specifically calibrated for and run in each focus community.
What makes this approach particularly powerful is its practical applicability. The modeling can identify temperature variations of 2030 degrees within distances as short as 2030 feet, helping communities identify immediate solutions even before interventions are implemented. Unlike traditional heat mapping that might simply show an area as a "red spot," this approach can pinpoint specific cool areas, such as those near creeks or under tree canopy. This is especially effective in the Central Valley's dry climate. The models are validated through strategically placed sensors that measure temperature, humidity, and globe temperature, creating wet bulb globe temperature equivalents at the meter level.
Perhaps most importantly, the technical approach is deeply integrated with community knowledge and needs. Models are developed in continuous dialogue with communities, incorporating hyperlocal demographic and behavioral input  such as identifying frequently used bus stops or gathering spaces. This integration helps prioritize intervention locations and provides immediately actionable information for community members. The approach effectively avoids the paralysis often induced by traditional "doom and gloom" heat mapping by identifying specific, achievable improvements. Rather than presenting communities with overwhelming, tractlevel heat data, it provides meterlevel resolution that's particularly wellsuited for rural and mixeduse communities. This combination of technical sophistication and community integration creates a powerful tool for understanding and addressing urban heat islands at a truly local level.
1.  **Bulletpoint form**:
1.  Data Sources and Resolution
    1.  Moving beyond conventional heat data sources (EcoStress, CalAdapt)
    2.  Key issue: Traditional sources lack sufficient resolution
    3.  Using multiple enhanced data sources: a. Local Climate Zones (LCZ) Classification
        1.  Detailed meteorological land use classification
        2.  Categories include farmland, city, open/dense subdivisions
        3.  More accurate than traditional classifications b. Population Data Integration:
        4.  Popgrid (Harvard)  Uses buildinglevel data
        5.  Microsoft building data layer integration
        6.  Distributes population estimates to actual buildings rather than census tracts
        7.   More precise than traditional census data c. RTI Synthetic Populations
        8.  Demographic data distributed to building locations
        9.  Uses mathematical approaches for greater precision
        10. Particularly useful for health data analysis
2.  Modeling Approach
    1.  Building simulation models for each neighborhood
    2.  Incorporates wind data for thermal comfort calculations
    3.  Creates Universal Thermal Climate Index measurements including:
        1.  Air temperature
        2.  Radiant temperature
        3.  Humidity
        4.  Wind
    4.  Achieves square meterlevel resolution for communities
    5.  Similar to wet bulb temperature but more granular
    6.  Models run specifically in focus communities.
3.  Practical Applications
    1.  Identifies temperature variations within small areas (2030 degree differences within 2030 feet)
    2.  Helps identify immediate solutions before interventions
    3.  More actionable than traditional "red spot" mapping
    4.  Can identify specific cool areas (e.g., near creeks, under trees)
    5.  Particularly effective in Central Valley's dry climate
4.  Validation Method
    1.  Deploying sensors to validate models
    2.  Sensors measure multiple variables:
        1.  Temperature
        2.  Humidity
        3.  Globe temperature
    3.  Creates wet bulb globe temperature equivalents
    4.  Validation at meterlevel resolution
5.  Community Integration
    1.  Models developed in dialogue with communities
    2.  Gathers hyperlocal demographic and behavioral input
    3.   Identifies specific community usage patterns (e.g., bus stop locations)
    4.  Helps prioritize intervention locations
    5.  Provides immediate actionable information for community members
6.  Advantages Over Traditional Approaches
    1.  Avoids "doom and gloom" paralysis of traditional heat mapping
    2.  Identifies specific actionable areas for improvement
    3.  Provides meterlevel resolution versus census tract data
    4.  Better suited for rural and mixeduse communities
    5.  Integrates community knowledge with technical data
This technical approach represents a significant advancement over traditional heat mapping methods, providing more detailed, actionable data while incorporating community needs and behaviors.
5\) How to present the Workplan?
  - Pick a clear framing: Our workplan accomplishes 4 things: list them and then provide bullet points for each category. The idea is to not get lost in the details and present it to OPR synthetically.
  - Here’s a potential way to frame it:
Our workplan accomplishes 4 key things:
1\. Data Collection & Analysis
  - Building a comprehensive sensor network across communities
  - Integrating groundlevel monitoring with advanced modeling
  - Creating digital twins for hyperlocal heat mapping
  - Combining multiple data sources (community sensors, satellite data, health data)
2\. Community Engagement & Training
  - Training community organizations in sensor deployment and data interpretation
  - Hosting community meetings to determine sensor placement and interventions
  - Developing culturally competent outreach strategies
  - Building capacity for longterm community science
3\. Technology Development & Implementation
  - Creating an extreme heat routing and notification app
  - Developing userfriendly data visualization tools
  - Integrating realtime data with predictive modeling
  - Building decisionsupport tools for communities
4\. NatureBased Solutions
  - Implementing targeted greening interventions
  - Installing pilot projects with monitoring capabilities
  - Measuring intervention effectiveness
  - Creating sustainable maintenance plans
This structure shows how the project moves from data collection through community engagement to actual interventions, while building sustainable infrastructure for future expansion.
Each component has clear deliverables, involves multiple partners, and builds toward the project's ultimate goal of creating a replicable model for communityled climate adaptation.
From Claude
Executive Summary:
The entire executive summary could be adapted to provide a highlevel overview of the Fresno project, highlighting the key objectives, approach, and anticipated outcomes.
Simulation:
The "Role of ENVImet" section could be repurposed to describe Hyphae's technical capabilities and the value of advanced modeling techniques for the Fresno project.
The "Inputs," "Outputs and implementation," and "Design Process, Collaboration and Iteration" sections provide valuable details on data requirements, expected deliverables, and stakeholder engagement that could be incorporated into the relevant sections of the Fresno proposal.
Monitoring and prepost study:
The overview of the monitoring approach and key findings from the CAMO project could be adapted to strengthen the case for the proposed monitoring and evaluation activities in Fresno.
Methods:
The entire Methods section, including subsections on Simulation, Design Process, and Monitoring, could be repurposed to provide a detailed description of Hyphae's technical approach and methodologies for the Fresno project.
Specific elements like the digital twin procedure, CFD meshing, meteorological inputs, traffic data integration, and simulation output metrics are directly relevant to the proposed vulnerability analysis and intervention design tasks.
The collaboration and iteration process outlined in the CAMO proposal aligns well with the stakeholder engagement and participatory design approach envisioned for the Fresno project.
Results:
While the specific results from the CAMO project may not be directly applicable, the structure and format of this section could be adapted to present the anticipated outcomes and deliverables for the Fresno project.
The subsections on thermal comfort simulation, pollution modeling, and monitoring results demonstrate Hyphae's ability to translate complex technical analyses into meaningful, actionable insights for decisionmakers and community stakeholders.
Appendices:
The literature review and annotated bibliography could be repurposed to demonstrate Hyphae's deep domain expertise and familiarity with best practices in urban heat mitigation, air quality monitoring, and naturebased solutions.
The glossary of terms and definitions could be adapted to ensure clarity and consistency in the Fresno proposal.
By systematically reviewing the CAMO proposal and identifying all the relevant sections, we can ensure that the Fresno RFP response leverages the full breadth of Hyphae's experience, technical capabilities, and proven methodologies. This comprehensive approach will result in a stronger, more compelling proposal that demonstrates Hyphae's unique qualifications and value proposition for the Fresno Extreme Heat project.
Here are the key sections from the CAMO proposal that can be adapted for the Fresno Extreme Heat RFP, along with notes on where they might fit:
Project Vision (for the Project Overview section):
"The vision of the California Environmental Justice Coalition is to form a communityled research observatory and task force capable of monitoring and mitigating the deadly effects of extreme heat in the Central and Imperial valleys. Our project, the Communityled Microclimate Observation Network (COMMON), aims to transform these regions into models of climate resilience, and brings a unique focus on farmworkerpopulated agricultural areas that are often left out of extreme heat remediation efforts directed towards cities."
Project Description (for the Project Overview section):
"COMMON aims to enhance the climate resilience of the San Joaquin and Imperial Valleys with communitydriven solutions. The following 30month implementation timeline combines communityled outreach, sensor installation, data collection, software development, and mitigation solutions."
(The detailed 3year timeline that follows could be adapted to fit the Fresno project scope and deliverables)
Community Needs and Priorities (for the Communities Served section):
"Though each of the subcontracted CEJC partners fights its own unique pollution challenges, we are all united in the fight against extreme heat. In order to adequately assess the vulnerability of our respective communities, however, we require data that has enough granularity to a) pinpoint high risk areas and b) correlate that vulnerability with health outcomes. With this hyperlocal data provided by our technical partners, we can then direct decisionmakers to implement projects, codes, standards, regulations, and policies that protect our populations from extreme heat."
(The detailed methodology for obtaining hyperlocal data and conducting the vulnerability analysis could be repurposed for Task 3)
Partnership Development (for the Large Grant Application section on engaging public entities): 
"As COMMON evolves, we fully intend to partner with tribes and local public authorities, as well as government agencies and public landowners. Though this phase of COMMON's evolution does not propose naturebased solutions on public property, we hope that in the future expansion of the Observation Network, we can secure public cooperation on a number of large implementation projects installed on public rightofways. These include installing green corridors along public transportation routes, 'green oases' protecting mobile home parks populated by farmworkers, and 'cool groves' that provide shade in agricultural hotspots."
CoBenefits (for the Projects with Multiple Benefits section):
"The proposed extreme heat monitoring and mitigation network offers a multifaceted approach to addressing the challenges posed by climate change, particularly for vulnerable communities. By generating hyperlocal predictions of biophysical heat stress and providing a userfriendly app, the project empowers community members to navigate extreme heat events safely. The installation of thermal stress sensors in neighborhoods and the provision of mobile sensors to farmworkers and other atrisk populations, when combined with health data from UCM will generate valuable data to better understand the impact of extreme heat on public health outcomes and healthcare costs."
These excerpts provide a strong starting point for the Fresno RFP narrative. The overall vision, communitydriven approach, technical methodologies, and emphasis on cobenefits articulated in the CAMO proposal are highly relevant and transferable. Some tailoring will be needed to adapt the specifics to the Fresno context and RFP requirements, but the core elements can be effectively repurposed.

  - Re: process, takeaways were

          - SJV Air needs to explain how much work Hyphae has already put into this without pay

*<comment_start id=kix.yse9r5btrk0f>Main Discussion Points<comment_end id=kix.yse9r5btrk0f>*
Planting Design Fundamentals
  - Merissa explained that planting plans are designed for future growth - trees need spacing to reach mature size
  - Current plan is already calculated for the available space using appropriate spacing
  - Simply adding more trees would lead to overcrowding, competition, and tree death as they mature
The "More Smaller Trees" Problem
  - Planting smaller trees of same species = same eventual size, still smae mount of trees needed
  - Benefit of larger trees: immediate buffer impact; smaller trees just delay effectiveness
  - AdaptOS modeling will show overcrowding if tree count is doubled
Possible Alternative: Columnar Species
  - Could potentially use narrow, columnar species (Arizona Cypress, Arborvitae) to fit more trees
  - Major concern: Buffer effectiveness requires varied species, shapes, and sizes for PM 2.5 capture
  - Switching to just 2-3 columnar species may compromise buffer function
Budget & Site Conditions
  - New site (integrated with Arvin sidewalk improvements) doesn't warrant budget changes
  - Same square footage, same prep requirements
  - Current budget remains appropriate

## May 15, 2025 | [Air District & Hyphae Team Meeting ](https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=XzYwcTMwYzFnNjBvMzBlMWk2MG80YWMxZzYwcmo4Z3BsODhyajJjMWg4NHMzNGg5ZzYwczMwYzFnNjBvMzBjMWc4OG80YWMyMTc0cDM2aDFuOGNxNDhnaGc2NG8zMGMxZzYwbzMwYzFnNjBvMzBjMWc2MG8zMmMxZzYwbzMwYzFnOGtwamFnaG04NG8zNGQ5bzhwMjNhZGhrNzUwajhlMW04Y3BrOGRwazhoMmpnaDFuNjkyMCBtYXhAaHlwaGFlLm5ldA)
Attendees: [Juan Penaloza Gutierrez](mailto:juan@hyphae.net) [Brent Bucknum](mailto:brent@hyphae.net) [Maxwell Pingeon](mailto:max@hyphae.net) [David Lopez](mailto:david.lopez@valleyair.org) [Nancy Rios](mailto:nancy.rios@valleyair.org) [Sarah Hasan Lane](mailto:sarah.hasanlane@valleyair.org) [Stephanie Ng](mailto:stephanie.ng@valleyair.org)
**Topic: Vegetative Buffer Project Strategy and Site Selection for CSC Review**
#### **I. Site Review and Target Locations**
The meeting began with a focused review of potential buffer sites in **Arvin** and **Lamont**. In Arvin, the team reaffirmed that the **Comanche Drive corridor**—especially near Bear Mountain Elementary—was the strongest candidate due to its alignment with CSC feedback, adjacency to almond groves (raising pesticide drift concerns), and favorable wind conditions. In Lamont, leading sites included the **Lamont Elementary School frontage** (within Caltrans right-of-way) and a **Kern County site near Weedpatch Park on Buena Vista Blvd**, both of which had prior community support.
Visuals were shared to demonstrate these zones, and the team discussed potential for pilot-style planting zones or "control" sites depending on final locations and funding flexibility.
#### **II. Maintenance Endowment and Long-Term Viability**
The conversation returned repeatedly to the **challenge of long-term maintenance**, with participants emphasizing the need to either secure a commitment from local institutions (e.g., schools, County) or **scale up funding to seed a reliable endowment**. Brent Bucknum reiterated that a 10-year maintenance commitment remains the key barrier to implementation and credibility. Caltrans's **Adopt-a-Highway model** was discussed as a partial solution, though it would still require a responsible party to step in post-installation.
#### **III. Incongruities in the Current RFP Structure**
Participants voiced concerns about structural misalignments in the current RFP process—most notably, the **absence of a funded design and planning phase**. As Bucknum noted, “this is not how it should have happened.” Normally, a design and engineering scope would precede implementation funding. The team has already performed substantial early-phase work without compensation and cannot continue doing so indefinitely.
This raised a broader question: how do we **educate CSC members on what typical infrastructure development entails**, including siting, utilities (e.g., 4-1-1 calls), tree species selection, and irrigation planning? As Brent emphasized, helping the community understand these phases is a critical piece of their long-term civic power: “It’s really helpful to be able to be like, ‘Here’s what gets decided at each process.’”
#### **IV. Budget Constraints and Feasibility of Two Sites**
Stephanie Ng asked whether $500K would be enough to support **smaller plantings in both Arvin and Lamont**. The team expressed skepticism. Key challenges include:
  - **Duplicated costs**: mobilizing two crews, paying multiple water connection fees (up to $25K per meter), and separate traffic control needs  
  - **Irrigation and utility coordination  
    **
  - **Lack of bandwidth to reprice everything from scratch without contract support  
    **
While a two-site strategy remains *possible*, it would either result in significantly reduced planting density or require supplemental funds (e.g., from the Urban Greening pot).
#### **V. Next Steps and Subcommittee Plan**
A **vegetative barrier subcommittee meeting** will be scheduled in the coming weeks. Hyphae and partners will propose **two candidate sites per city**—not as final selections, but as vetted options for CSC input. CSC members will be invited to **prioritize one site per city** for further investigation.
Key process elements:
  - Frame site selection as **community-guided, not predetermined  
    **
  - Provide **maps and a concise Google Doc or presentation** to share with CSC co-leads ahead of time  
  - Use the subcommittee meeting to secure a recommendation from CSC members that allows contracting to move forward  
  - Clarify that even preferred sites are subject to feasibility (e.g., water access, construction cost)  
#### **VI. Recommendation for Future RFP Structure**
To avoid repeating this situation, Bucknum recommended that the Air District **issue a small, dedicated planning and design contract—perhaps 10% of total implementation funding**—in future RFPs. This would allow proper vetting and collaborative design before locking in site locations and budgets.
Rough Notes
  - Showing of sites
  - Discussion of maintenance endowments 
  - Structural incongruities in the RFP combining design and planning 
  - Question of doing smaller plantings in both Arvin and Lamont for $500k
      - Not really capacity to price out the contractor and installation costs 
      - Questions of water, irrigation, other constraints, deploying two crews at once 
      - Doubling costs becomes unsustainable for that amount of $
  - Re: the sub-committee meeting:
      - Propose 1 from each
          - But clarify that it’s an either/or?
              - And CSC can choose 
          - If CSC supports it, can move forward 
          - Co-leads can get downloaded to vet the sites and work with CSC members online prior to the meeting 
              - Put together a prez or google doc that can be shared 
      - We need to explain to the community that this is not how things typically get built: the planning phase needs to precede the implementation phase 
          - AD: Want to also show the CSC that their work has been fruitful 
          - Should the CSC approve, can begin contracting 
          - BB recommendation to issue a 10% planning RFP

Attendees: [Juan Penaloza Gutierrez](mailto:juan@hyphae.net) [Brent Bucknum](mailto:brent@hyphae.net) [Maxwell Pingeon](mailto:max@hyphae.net) [David Lopez](mailto:david.lopez@valleyair.org) [Nancy Rios](mailto:nancy.rios@valleyair.org) [Sarah Hasan Lane](mailto:sarah.hasanlane@valleyair.org) [Stephanie Ng](mailto:stephanie.ng@valleyair.org)
Notes
  - AD moving on with City of Kern project but leaving door open for future collaborations
      - And consideration for additional funding 
      - Hyphae can pause on Shafter
  - Transitioning to A-L
      - CSC wants type of projects 
      - Relationship building 
      - BB gave a quick overview
      - Caltrans eager to participate and to share about their process
      - Re: Shafter, the opportunities fore synerguies  Stewardsip a
  - What is the length of the A-a-H sponsorship?
  - Ag landowner cooperation can become complicated bec their costs cut into the tree budget 
      - BB: hard to 
  - Ask:
      - How do you masterplan these infrastructure changes prior to the implementation phase ?
  - Response:
      - Not sure if they are going a long-term vegetated buffers program bec doesn;t reduce emissions in a significant way  
      - Tree planting is more of a city and municipal public works responsibility
      - But w/in AB 617, we could do smaller projects as pilots to grow into it, to be supplemented by future rounds of funding 
          - Gave the oleander example from Shafter 
          - Possibly scale up the funding from an additional $500k “urban greening” pot that’s coming down the pike
          - This was requested from the CSC
          - CSC wants barriers in both communities: we can consider pulling from this urban greening $
              - Pulls from possible urban greening improvements that the community also want
      - Re: upcoming subcommittee meeting, we need a few ideas
          - Show that behind-the-scenes expertise
          - Have a distinct proposal          
Take-aways 
Stephanie clarified that the **Air District does not foresee a long-term, Valley-wide vegetative barrier or urban greening program** beyond what AB 617 currently funds. While she acknowledged the community value and co-benefits of such projects, she emphasized that **urban greening does not yield high enough emissions reductions to align with the District’s core regulatory mandate**.
That said, she expressed openness to supporting **targeted, scaled-down greening efforts** in **Arvin and Lamont**, especially if project scope and cost were reduced—for example, by proposing smaller initial plantings that could grow over time. In this context, she floated the possibility of tapping into an **existing $500K urban greening pot** as a potential source of supplementary funding, if budget constraints made it impossible to fund two discrete projects under the current AB 617 allocation.
Regarding the **maintenance endowment**, Stephanie did not directly commit to supporting the concept. Instead, she reiterated that **long-term maintenance is ideally the responsibility of municipalities**, not the Air District. However, she welcomed further dialogue and emphasized that **any future support would need to be framed clearly as part of the limited AB 617 scope**, with no expectation of long-term expansion.
In sum, while the District cannot commit to a systemic funding model for vegetative barriers or long-term maintenance, Stephanie signaled conditional support for near-term, community-supported greening projects—**if kept modest in scale and aligned with AB 617 priorities**
We scheduled a meeting for the following day to further discuss.

## May 1, 2025 | [City of Arvin prep](https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=NnMxdmdmNTQ2NmI3NWE0NzNpcDJwb3RhNzggbWF4QGh5cGhhZS5uZXQ) 
Attendees: Sam Lux, Maxwell Pingeon, Brent, Christine Viterelli
I. <comment_start id=kix.ql9bvylzsmsu>Project Integration Strategy<comment_end id=kix.ql9bvylzsmsu>
Brent Bucknum opened with an overview of the barrier concept, emphasizing that the goal was to integrate tree planting buffers into existing city-funded sidewalk and curb improvement projects. 
Sam Lux presented the three major AB 617-funded infrastructure projects currently underway in Arvin (see screenshots below):
1.  *Site \#1: A Street Corridor* (From Bear Mountain Blvd to Olson)
  - Question: Both sides of street? 
  - A good Plan B with the two schools: the middle and elementary school
      - Sam identified the A Street corridor near Sierra Vista Elementary as good for construction coordination, and Brent acknowledged it as a practical fallback
          - even if it’s less impacted by pesticide drift than Comanche.
  
\[image\]
1.  Site \#2: Tahoe/Derby Corridor (Derby/Tahoe from Orange to Sycamore on West side only – includes bike lanes)
\[image\]  
1.  Site \#3: Curb, gutter and sidewalk on East side of Comanche (from unnamed street just north of Schipper to Shane Court)
1.  See green line below 
1.  (Note that the Comanche project is split into two sections: this is section 1, the much longer of the two.) 
1.  This is the priority site (Plan A), with a 400ft barrier approx buffering the school with opportunities for a control site to the North or South.
\[image\]
1.  Second section: East side of Comanche, from Varsity Ave south for 522 ft.
1.  See green line
\[image\]  
All are funded, approved, and undergoing surveying using a wearable Navis LiDAR scanner. 
There is also ongoing commercial and real estate development in the area 
1.  Gas station (red box)
\[image\]
\[image\]
1.  Mini storage (red box)
 
\[image\]
\[image\]
Both have certain landscape requirements and could thus share curb and gutter installation burden: 
  - Could be a Plan C if we wanted to stack functions with them: i.e. allow our buffer to satisfy their landscape requirements
  - With them managing the site, there’s an opportunity to do more innovative planting. 
-----
II. Site Viability and Agricultural Buffer Targeting
Brent and Max explained Hyphae's current task: 
  - Buffer pesticide and dust
  - Assessing sites identified by the community through AB 617 for their feasibility (ROW width, setbacks, Caltrans constraints, property lines, etc.). 
  - Derby was submitted for initial grant purposes, but Comanche emerged as a higher priority in community feedback due to concerns about pesticide drift from adjacent almond orchards.
Re: outreach to farmers:
  - BB recounted Gustavo’s report of past failed efforts to secure farmer buy-in or even to reach the relevant point of contact 
      - Better to take a different approach than through AB 617: engage the Ag commissioner maybe, water districts, Ag extension or some other network
  - Sam endorsed this strategy
      - Saw alignment insofar as Agricultural Commissioner is likely eager to mitigate community pressure regarding pesticide drift and notification issues. 
  - He suggested engaging the Ag Commissioner directly as a politically effective conduit to growers—"it would look better if the idea came from him." 
  - Brent agreed and noted this approach could also satisfy Valley Air District’s and County’s preference for on-farm maintenance and irrigation buy-in.
  - Brent and Sam agreed that we could plant our intervention on Site \#3 buffering the school on Comanche with a designated control site either to the North or South.
      - This placement would leverage prevailing westerly winds for study design.
  - Re: planting logistics
      - Utility lines can be avoided with denser columnar evergreen conifers that top out at different heights, or that can be topped.
  - The trees are essentially managed more like a hedge than a tree. 
-----
III. Technical and Design Coordination
  - Christine reiterated that tree planting must be integrated into existing City projects (either Comanche, Derby, or South A Street).  
  - Sam Lux should manage it as part of his current scope.  
  - City can’t support new standalone sites due to added construction management costs.  
  - Grant is going to Hyphae, so City won’t be reimbursed for extra work.  
  - No planting outside project areas unless budget covers City’s role.
  - Brent agreed the goal is to **work within existing City projects** to avoid extra lift.
  - Emphasized that Hyphae is now ready to **collaborate directly with Sam** on site selection.
  - Clarified that if **additional design or engineering costs arise**, Hyphae can **cover them from its existing design scope**.
  - Cited a similar Oakland project where they budgeted funds for City staff participation (e.g., stormwater, traffic engineers).
  - Reiterated that **integrating into existing City work is their preferred and budgeted approach**.
  - Hyphae to handle **irrigation and landscape design**, integrating it directly into the City’s project sets.  
  - Hyphae would **use the City’s survey and design drawings** to produce the necessary landscape package—no extra lift for City staff.  
  - This approach avoids additional overhead and shows **grant dollars going further**.  
  - Brent suggested Hyphae could act as a **subconsultant**, making the integration seamless.
  - Sam confirmed that none of the sidewalk projects currently include tree planting or irrigation scopes, but he is open to integration if aligned with active construction efforts.  
  - Brent proposed that Hyphae manage irrigation and landscape design as a subconsultant, based on similar precedent in Oakland.  
  - Sam agreed, noting that integration would streamline inspections since the same team would oversee both scopes.  
  - Christine reiterated that any greening work outside current project areas is not feasible unless additional permitting and CM funds are provided.  
  - Sam flagged that active development along Tejon/Sycamore (e.g., gas station, mini storage, residential builds) makes that corridor unavailable for planting.  
  - He identified the A Street corridor near Sierra Vista Elementary as ideal for construction coordination, even though it may be less impacted by pesticide drift.  
  - Brent and Christine agreed that A Street could serve as a fallback site if Comanche becomes unworkable.  
-----
### **IV. Data Tools and Reporting**
  - Sam shared that the City is using a **Navis wearable LiDAR scanner** for current surveying.  
  - Brent offered **technical support from Hyphae’s team (e.g., Fletch)** to process the LiDAR data, referencing their past experience with similar tree canopy mapping.  
  - Christine confirmed that **tree location, species, and canopy spread** data will be required for **urban greening grant reporting** (e.g., GHG sequestration, tree inventory).  
  - Sam and Brent agreed the LiDAR will support **streamlined integration with the City’s existing tree canopy plan**, minimizing manual documentation work.  
-----
### **V. Next Steps**
  - The group agreed to **prioritize Comanche (Section 1) as the intervention site**, with **A Street as Plan B** and **Tahoe/Derby as fallback Plan C**.  
  - Comanche offers the strongest alignment with **community priorities (drift buffer)** and **opportunities for paired control site analysis**.  
  - Christine departed early for an **AB 617 agenda-setting meeting**, asking for an update on whether materials will be ready for the **June meeting**.
-----

## Apr 30, 2025 | [Hyphae x Kern County](https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=NTJkMGoyZnRvODZlN2k4dDdhMzNpNXBjaG8gbWF4QGh5cGhhZS5uZXQ)
Attendees: [Brent Bucknum](mailto:brent@hyphae.net) [Maxwell Pingeon](mailto:max@hyphae.net) [](mailto:penj@kerncounty.com) <comment_start id=kix.xjrex7cv5xnl>[](mailto:yolandar@kerncounty.com)<comment_end id=kix.xjrex7cv5xnl>
Attached files: <comment_start id=kix.uwrwg2bc1647>[Vegetative Barrier-notes\&policy.pdf](https://drive.google.com/file/d/1lFs2XkRAtxnieb6PYAoNZBSPqywpEvCY/view?usp=drive_link)<comment_end id=kix.uwrwg2bc1647> [Shafter Veg Buff RFP proposal](https://docs.google.com/document/d/18exRwsHDaDnEfw71PgT2ygMUtXs0Ei20Y5XwMN6VwHs/edit?tab=t.0) 
I. Vegetative Barrier Maintenance and Ownership Concerns
Meeting opened with Yolanda Alcantar (YA) reiterating Kern County’s longstanding concerns regarding long-term maintenance. 
Drawing from past failures (e.g., Northchester median trees), she emphasized that:
  - O\&M agreements are often ineffective without allocated funding contingencies.
  - A prior example—the Northchester beautification project—failed when the irrigation system’s electrical components broke. The Rec District couldn't afford repairs, the trees died, and now the County must remove them at its own expense.
  - The County lacks the ability to use general road maintenance funds for greening. They can support partnerships with landowners but cannot assume fiscal responsibility unless specified in an agreement.
  - Public perception is often unaware of this fundamental gap in funding—residents request landscaping or trees, unaware that maintenance is unfunded.
YA also voiced **frustration with the Valley Air District (SJVAPCD), noting that the agency has accumulated millions of dollars in mitigation fees from oil and gas permits, yet has failed to release those funds for local greening or infrastructure projects**. 
Despite repeated requests, the County has been unable to access these funds.
Brent Bucknum (BB) agreed that maintenance is the critical barrier to success in greening efforts and outlined a strategic approach to negotiation with the AD in light of structural problems in the RFP.
  - RFP funds a 3-year project term but requires a 10-year maintenance commitment, with no provision for long-term O\&M.
  - In our proposal, Hyphae submitted placeholder sites (in Arvin and Shafter, but not Lamont) to meet proposal deadlines, but does not plan to plant there unless they prove optimal. Valley Air has since expressed openness to funding multiple smaller sites in Arvin and Lamont if better options emerge.
  - Our proposal seeks to pilot a maintenance endowment model, allocating 40% of the budget to long-term care. This would involve an initial $300K drawdown potentially managed by the San Joaquin Community Foundation, with future projects contributing to scale the fund.
  - Brent suggested that the County submit a formal statement or letter to the Air District documenting the barriers it faces, to support advocacy for structural reform.
Possible template for collaborative maintenance idea involving the County:
  - YA referenced a model MOU with the City of Bakersfield, in which the City agreed to maintain the improvements. She offered to share that agreement with Hyphae.
      - **YA:** *“We have a model where the City of Bakersfield was able to put an MOU together with us, and it included that the city would maintain it. So I can share that one with you.” *(timestamp \~28:32))
Then Brent expanded on our Adopt-a-Highway strategy:
  - BB offered to share Caltrans materials detailing design and maintenance agreements for ROW planting.
  - The County could explore this framework as a model for vegetation buffers, particularly where long-term maintenance responsibilities may be shared with adjacent landowners.
-----
II. Approaching Agricultural Landowners
All agreed that outreach must be non-political and benefits-focused to avoid triggering defensiveness:
  - Brent emphasized using trusted intermediaries—Resource Conservation Districts or Ag Extension Officers—rather than more politicized AB 617 groups.
  - YA confirmed that ag landowners are often more receptive to mutually beneficial framing: dust control, habitat, shielding crops from view, and privacy.
  - In years past, the County secured a right-of-entry agreement on Comanche Road to pave the shoulder. YA will check with the right-of-way team to confirm whether contact information still exists for that landowner.
      - **YA:** *“We did do one—we had one agreement where we did a right of entry to pave the shoulder. So I’ll check with our right-of-way folks to see if they’ve got a contact with that landowner.” *(Recording timestamp 44:45)
BB raised whether the County could serve as a convener, with Hyphae supporting outreach, if asked.
-----
III. Site Locations and Compatibility with County Projects
Arvin
  - Hyphae’s placeholder site was submitted between Sycamore and El Camino Real (Tejon Hwy). BB emphasized, however, that this is not a preferred location—it was simply the only viable option known at submission.
  - Other potential sites we discussed section of Tejon at the intersection of Capistrano Street near Bear Mountain Elementary School, the latter being an option for on-campus planting to avoid ROW constraints. Notably, though identified as priority in previous AB 617 meetings, as reported by JO, the RFP language only shows sites south of the area as prioritized by community.
 
  - For sites with less than Caltrans’s 30-ft setback requirement. Brent suggested:
  - Using shrubs (Caltrans allows smaller setbacks with appropriate planting).
  - Installing curbs, which can reduce required setbacks—City of Arvin has funding for curb/gutter/sidewalk work. Caltrans allows smaller setbacks if a curb is present
Lamont – Projects Identified by Kern County
1.  Buena Vista Blvd (ARPA-funded) ending in a park (see screenshots below)
1.  Panama Road & Neighborhood Streets (see screenshots below)
  - Full sidewalk buildout north of Panama (Carnation to Collision/Montoya/Baratel).
  - Panama Rd: Sidewalks on north side from Carnation to Main, switching to south side westward.
1.  Mountain View (north of Panama Rd – see screenshots below)
      - Sidewalk improvements west of Sherman Rd to the railroad. May be suitable for school access and Ag buffer (south side).
YA noted design drawings are not yet finalized. Engineering teams began new designs in early 2025, following completion of a $50M ARPA project that occupied most staff through December 2024. She will share with Hyphae what work has been done so far.  
-----
IV. Shafter Coordination and Site Vetting
Mexican Colony (SJV Air project – see screenshots below)
  - Sidewalk construction begins Spring 2026 with AD money. 
  - County pursuing rehab and bike lanes on Burbank to the North and then extending North on Shafter to City limits
  - Hyphae proposed to plant on the community-nominated Route 43 running alongside Cherokee Strip, on Caltrans-controlled land.
Brent noted challenges working with Caltrans but suggested Adopt-a-Highway as a workaround.
Road ownership clarity:
  - YA confirmed that most roads in Shafter are under city control.
  - She recommended reaching out to Michael James (City of Shafter), calling him a strong and responsive partner.
-----
V. Action Items & Responsibilities
- [x] MP: Send Caltrans A-a-H guidelines to Kern County
- [x] MP: request project reports and agreements from County re: sidewalk improvements
- [x] MP: write up meeting notes
- [x] MP: circulate meeting notes 
- [x] MP: Mark ongoing County projects in Felt
- [x] MP: Request MOU County signed with City of Bakersfield that includes O\&M terms.
- [x] MP: Request if County can confirm any existing contact with Comanche Rd landowner and related ROW team from previous project.
- [ ] MP: Share our letter to the AD with the County outlining our strategy.
- [ ] YA (TBD): Consider submitting their own letter to Valley Air documenting maintenance constraints and funding challenges.
- [x] BB: Refine engagement strategy with Valley Air to push for structural reform and multiple-site pilot approval.

## Apr 30, 2025 | [Caltrans District 6 x Hyphae](https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=M3ZsYXM2bnM2dHBrNWc0ZDVxOG9idWlxZnMgbWF4QGh5cGhhZS5uZXQ)
Attendees: [Adair, Jason@DOT](mailto:jason.adair@dot.ca.gov) [Brent Bucknum](mailto:brent@hyphae.net) [Maxwell Pingeon](mailto:max@hyphae.net)
I. <comment_start id=kix.yf2azpk5ai91>Clarification<comment_end id=kix.yf2azpk5ai91> of Sites and Grants
  - Arvin and Lamont: Hyphae has secured the grant but site in limbo. Community feedback voiced preference for barriers on Comanche side of town to buffer the school (rather than the initial Tejon site we proposed).  
  - Shafter: Still in limbo—Hyphae submitted a proposal but lacked Caltrans approval for state-owned land, which the Air District considers essential. The City of Shafter and a local school submitted a competing application, but it's unclear how much of the budget they requested.  
II. Caltrans’s Role and Strategic Path Forward
  - Jason Adair (Caltrans) explicitly affirmed the<comment_start id=kix.ycgbm6t11oiz> best path forward<comment_end id=kix.ycgbm6t11oiz> is to:  
    1.  Submit an Adopt-A-Highway proposal (rather than seeking a formal Caltrans project), escalate to encroachment permit only if required. Ideally, only small sections of the project would require encroachment permitting.
1.  Submit a fact sheet showcasing the District 10 approach taken in Stockton: include photos and specific postmile references to show precedent and ease concerns.  
2.  Avoid initiating a PDT (Project Development Team) process in District 6 if possible, as it would significantly delay things. At some later point, however, District 6 could potentially benefit from seeing how District 10 operates, particularly as they both work in similarly arid conditions and thus similar irrigation constraints.   
III. Fact Sheet Request and Use
  - Jason requested:  
      - A <comment_start id=kix.c1swdfpp1iao>fact sheet<comment_end id=kix.c1swdfpp1iao> that reads as:
  - We’re bringing a solution not a problem 
  - We’re handling the maintenance – finding the water etc
  - Show that we have success in a similarly arid district (e.g. success of being awarded the Stockton project in District 10) 
  - And that this was the advice of District 10 landscape architect Escutia, Gus@DOT \<<gus.escutia@dot.ca.gov>\>
See also:
  - Del Pozo, Antonio@DOT \<<Antonio.Del.Pozo@dot.ca.gov>\>
  - El-Nakhal, Dina@DOT \<<dina.el.nakhal@dot.ca.gov>\>,
  - Sanguinetti, Tanya@DOT \<Tanya.Sanguinetti@dot.ca.gov\>,
  - Powell-Jones, Brenda M@DOT <brenda.powell-jones@dot.ca.gov>
  - Ponce, Gregoria@DOT \<<gregoria.ponce@dot.ca.gov>
  - Others?
 
  - Show that we know about Caltrans needs (timestamp 30:09 - 31:25 in transcript):
      - “Driver recovery zones”
      - “Maintenance vehicle issues” 
      - “traffic safety requirements”
  - Include names of Caltrans landscape architects and others (Hilda Sousa, planner e.g.) 
Include success stories of Diana Gomez and Tree Fresno as precedent 
  - Gomez retired in December - Michael Navarro her successor since February
  - See also Jason Miao Office Chief of Maintenance Caltrans – District 6, (559) 341-7990 jason.miao@dot.ca.gov)
  - Also include:
      - Specific post-mile segments
      - Site photos, maps
      - Evidence of community support  
  - JA: ‘Hey, we did an Adopt-A-Highway on these post miles in this state route in this district. Here's what it did. Here's some photos. Here's what we did. We went through this process. Here's our contacts at District 10, District 3...’
  - Chat-drafted sentence:
      - Preliminary design for the proposed vegetative barrier along \[State Route XXX, postmiles XX–XX\] has been developed by licensed landscape architects and civil engineers, referencing Caltrans ROW standards and driver recovery zone specifications. Final planting plans will follow Adopt-A-Highway guidelines unless an encroachment permit is requested.
  - Hyphae will deliver this by Wednesday, May 7, ahead of Jason’s May 8 one-on-one with the acting deputy director of planning in District 6.  
  - Jason intends to present this fact sheet during the upcoming AB617 Arvin Lamont community meeting ([scheduled for 5/28/25](https://community.valleyair.org/selected-communities/arvin-lamont/steering-committee-meetings/)), signaling Caltrans's formal endorsement of the project and easing public concerns.  
IV. GIS / KMZ Request
  - Brent requested a KMZ or shapefile of Caltrans ROW for District 6 to confirm land ownership boundaries.  
  - Jason acknowledged the need and agreed to request it through the appropriate Caltrans channels.  
V. Action Timeline & Strategy
  - Hyphae will:  
      - Develop a [fact sheet.](https://dot.ca.gov/caltrans-near-me/district-4/d4-projects/d4-37-corridor-projects/37-projects/ea-04-2k740-factsheet-06302020)  
      - Submit Adopt-A-Highway applications for both [Arvin/Lamont](https://na2.documents.adobe.com/public/esignWidget?wid=CBFCIBAA3AAABLblqZhCmlBHF4SbC6Yujr_WTm5W4K8UypjIT4uqbgU2Dwp6LBaAPUtc4_kL7TFh51sZ5IWg*) and [Shafter](https://na2.documents.adobe.com/public/esignWidget?wid=CBFCIBAA3AAABLblqZhCmlBHF4SbC6Yujr_WTm5W4K8UypjIT4uqbgU2Dwp6LBaAPUtc4_kL7TFh51sZ5IWg*).  
      - Meet with Valley Air next week (5/7 9AM) to brief them on this strategy and coordinate inclusion of Caltrans (Jason) on the agenda.  
      - Use the [May 12 Shafter AB617 meetin](https://community.valleyair.org/selected-communities/shafter/steering-committee-meetings/)g to socialize the Caltrans plan and move the project forward.  
  - Jason will:  
      - Review and advocate for the Adopt-A-Highway path during his May 8 meeting with the Deputy District Director, Michael Navarro.  
      - Add the item to May 28 Arvin/Lamont AB617 [steering committee meeting](https://community.valleyair.org/selected-communities/arvin-lamont/steering-committee-meetings/) agenda to show community buy-in.  
      - Provide a sample Caltrans fact sheet for formatting reference.  
VI. Strategic Framing  
  - Hyphae isn’t asking for permission; it's offering a vetted, precedent-based solution.  
  - This supports Caltrans’ own strategic plan goals (community benefit, adaptation, EJ).  
  - Adopt-A-Highway is not new statewide, just new to District 6—thus can be normalized by showing it’s already happening elsewhere.

### **<comment_start id=kix.95q0hcrm7xcd>BRIEFING MEMO – ARVIN-LAMONT VEGETATIVE BUFFER PROJECT<comment_end id=kix.95q0hcrm7xcd>**
**Prepared for:** Brent Bucknum  
 **Prepared by:** Maxwell Pingeon  
 **Date:** 4/29/25  
 **Meeting Time:** 11:00 AM PT
-----
#### **Summary of Current Status**
  - **Funding:** $500,000 awarded from *San Joaquin Valley Air Pollution Control District* to plant vegetative buffers in Arvin and Lamont.  
  - **Original Plan:** Project proposal was aligned with City of Arvin’s sidewalk/gutter improvement plan in a previously community-identified high-concern area.  
  - **Recent Development:** Steering Committee approved the grant **but rejected the proposed site** in favor of a western section of Arvin **owned by Kern County**.  
  - **New Partnerships:  
    **
      - Kern County (owns new priority site)  
      - Caltrans (owns key right-of-ways along Route 184/Sycamore and Route 223/Main Street)  
  - **Upcoming Meetings:  
    **
      - **Caltrans – Wednesday Morning  
        **
      - **Kern County – Wednesday Afternoon  
        **
      - Goal: Assess feasibility of planting on newly identified sites and understand partner constraints and opportunities.
      - We also have a meeting with City of Arvin on Thursday 3:30PM PT   
-----
#### **Preparation Goals for Meetings**
  - **Main Objective:** Present viable vegetative buffer options on sites now prioritized by the community and assess partner willingness to support implementation.  
-----
#### **Site Overview**
  - **Arvin – West Side of South Comanche Drive (Kern County)  
    **
      - Community preference based on **pesticide exposure from adjacent almond orchards**.  
      - Proximity to schools and residential zones.  
      - **Advantages:** Long, uninterrupted stretch; ideal for continuous buffer without breaks (driveways/intersections).  
      - **Next Step:** Confirm land ownership extent, buffer width feasibility, and proximity to sensitive receptors.  
  - **Lamont – Main Street (Route 184, Caltrans)  
    **
      - Site near **Lamont Elementary School**, identified in community meetings.  
      - More segmented urban fabric with driveways and access points, reducing effectiveness of linear buffer.  
      - **Next Step:** Explore whether school district and Caltrans would support a contiguous planting along their respective right-of-ways.  
-----
#### **Data/Visuals to Have on Hand**
  - [**Wind rose data**](?tab=t.0#heading=h.sy3hvm7jfltt) from Bakersfield/Tejon Pass (shows dominant NW wind patterns supporting West Arvin siting).  
  - Maps showing:  
      - Sensitive receptors (schools, residential zones)  
      - Community-identified sites  
      - Jurisdiction boundaries (City of Arvin, Kern County, Caltrans ROW)  
  - Estimated buffer length (e.g., 0.35 mi = \~1,800 ft) and preliminary cost feasibility.  
  - Any Felt maps or AdaptOS overlays showing environmental burden.  
-----
#### **Outstanding Questions for Brent**
1.  Do you want us to prepare alternate site suggestions or just vet the ones prioritized by the community?
    1.  Discussions are  the vetting process. Don't spend time before 
    2.  <comment_start id=kix.mcj7eycc92og>Caltrans<comment_end id=kix.mcj7eycc92og> we're talking to about shafter right?  
2.  Should we prepare an estimate using **Plant Packer** or stick with linear footage + unit cost for now?
    1.  No. Not now. Wait til we have sites.  
3.  Do you want slides or static maps prepared for the partner meetings?
    1.  We have a presentation we gave. We're showing that and will have the felt board for discussion.  
4.  Should we reach out to the **school district(s)** ahead of Wednesday?
    1.  Why necessary now? 
    2.  Didn't we also ask the steering committee and valley air to connect us with other viable sites, while in the meeting?
    3.  This should be a valley air/community task in my mind.
5.  How should we prep for Arvin given that the community essentially rejected us doing work on the East side of town.
    1.  We haven't met with them and haven't even looked at where they want to do it. Where there project is. That's the purpose of the meeting 
    2.  The Eastern Side was kind and if random spot that we selected. 
6.  In general I feel like it is not the time to throw all these hyphae peoptat this, trying to solve things we don't know how to solve yet because we don't know where we can plant. we just gotta have these meetings.
-----

# <comment_start id=kix.3e0jsaorpwkn>Budget Justification for Air District<comment_end id=kix.3e0jsaorpwkn> (2/3/2025)
**Arvin/Lamont Vegetated Barrier - Contracted Services Budget Breakdown**
**Total Contracted Services:** $309,420
-----
### **1. Tree Sourcing & Delivery ($143,370)**
**Contractor: Devil Mountain Nursery**
  - **Tree Costs:** $121,500 (81 trees @ $1,500 each, including mulch, and 24” or 36” boxes)
  - **Delivery Fee:** $21,870 (18% of tree cost)
### **2. Planting Installation ($141,750)**
**Contractor: Platinum Pines**
  - **Crew Size:** 6-person team
  - **Equipment:** 2 machines (excavator/tree spade) operated by 2 crew members
  - **Estimated Duration:** Approximately 2 weeks (10 workdays)
  - **Estimated Crew Hours:** 480 total crew hours
  - **Task Breakdown & Estimated Costs:**
      - **Site Preparation (\~10%)** - Clearing, marking tree locations, minor grading (**$14,175**)
      - **Excavation & Hole Auguring (\~30%)** - Digging holes for 24" & 36" tree boxes using tree spade (**$42,525**)
      - **Tree Planting & Backfilling (\~40%)** - Transporting, planting, staking, and backfilling soil around trees (**$56,700**)
      - **Irrigation Hookup (\~20%)** - Connecting trees to City water supply, running drip lines, testing system (**$28,350**)
### **3. Establishment Maintenance ($18,000 over 3 years)**
**Contractor: Platinum Pines**
  - **Annual Cost: $6,000**
  - **Task Breakdown:**
  - Weekly waterings (40 weeks per year)
  - Monthly check-ups and clean-ups
  - Irrigation system maintenance (leak checks, adjustments)
  - Soil moisture monitoring and mulch replenishment
  - Pest and disease monitoring and treatment if needed
  - Weed control and general ground maintenance
  - Inspections after extreme weather events
  - Adjustments or removal of tree supports if necessary
### **4. Long-Term Maintenance ($6,300 over 7 years)**
**Contractor: Platinum Pines**
  - **Annual Cost:** $900 (15% of yearly establishment maintenance costs)
  - **Task Breakdown:**
      - **Quarterly irrigation system checks & adjustments**
      - **Bi-annual soil & mulch maintenance**
      - **Quarterly weed control & ground maintenance**
      - **Semi-annual pest & disease monitoring**
      - **Post-storm or extreme weather inspections**
**See Budget here: **[**Arvin/Lamont Vegetated Barrier RFP v2**](https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1mHGJ_LbYTkROtrk8ur9_q_wnElSrU-diUaORn4P5AV4/edit?gid=1977314034#gid=1977314034)** (make sure to click version 2 tab: “v2”) **
# Summary 
The **Arvin/Lamont Community Emissions Reduction Program: Vegetative Barriers RFP** provides $500,000 in funding to implement vegetative barriers that reduce air pollution and provide co-benefits like noise reduction, heat mitigation, and improved aesthetics within the Arvin/Lamont AB 617 Community. Projects must align with Community Steering Committee (CSC) priorities and are evaluated based on cost-effectiveness, proximity to sensitive receptors, co-benefits, species selection, and readiness. Applicants must use tools like WUCOLS IV, the DWR Water Budget Workbook, and the i-Tree Planting Tool to demonstrate environmental benefits, including the use of drought-tolerant, low-BVOC species. Successful projects must be implemented within three years and maintained for a minimum of 10 years, ensuring sustainable and measurable outcomes for this disadvantaged community.
# Proposal Components
1.  **Application Form**:
      - Completed form (Exhibit A in the RFP).
2.  **Workplan**:
      - Detailed project summary.
      - Implementation steps and timeline (design, permitting, installation, and maintenance).
3.  **Budget**:
      - Itemized cost breakdown (supplies, labor, contracted services, etc.).
      - Include detailed quotes and justifications.
      - Ensure adherence to funding allocations (e.g., 25% limit for non-construction costs, 10% contingency).
4.  **Attachments and Supporting Documentation**:
      - **CNRA Urban Greening Benefits Calculator Tool** results:
          - Include the "GHG Summary" and "Co-benefit Summary" tabs.
      - **Vegetation Planning Tools**:
          - **WUCOLS IV Tool**: Use this to classify water requirements for selected plant species.
          - **i-Tree Planting Calculator**: Provide estimates for CO₂ storage, energy savings, and pollution reductions.
          - **DWR Water Budget Workbook**:
              - Complete for both pre-project and post-project scenarios.
              - Include the **Maximum Applied Water Allowance (MAWA)** and **Estimated Total Water Use (ETWU)** results.
      - **Site Details**:
          - Photographs of the project site.
          - Aerial image from an online mapping tool.
          - Project site plan or representation.
      - **Certifications Form**:
          - Signed acknowledgment of compliance with project guidelines.
-----
**Additional Submission Requirements**
  - **Property Ownership or Authorization**:
      - If not the property owner, provide documentation proving authorization to conduct the project.
  - **Sensitive Receptor Identification**:
      - List sensitive receptors (e.g., schools, residences) within 1,000 feet of the project site.
  - **Species Selection**:
      - Include rationale for selecting species based on environmental benefits (e.g., drought tolerance, low BVOC emissions).
  - **Irrigation System**:
      - Provide details on the type of irrigation system to be installed (e.g., drip, overhead spray).
  - **Certifications and Permits**:
      - Ensure compliance with CEQA and obtain necessary permits.
-----
**Workplan Requirements**
  - **Project Summary**: Brief description of the project, partners, implementation period, and expected outcomes.
  - **Timeline**: Key milestones for:
      - Preliminary design.
      - Environmental documentation and permitting.
      - Installation and maintenance commitments.
  - **Roles and Responsibilities**: List key individuals or organizations and their project roles.
  - **Measures for Timely Completion**: Steps to ensure the project adheres to the timeline.
-----
**Budget Requirements**
The budget must include an **itemized breakdown** of costs:
  - Supplies and materials (e.g., vegetation, irrigation equipment).
  - Labor and construction.
  - Contracted services.
  - Signage and interpretive aids.
  - Non-construction costs (e.g., design, permitting, outreach)—capped at 25%.
  - Contingency costs—capped at 10%.
Additionally:
  - Include **quotes** for all estimated costs.
  - Ensure the total funding request aligns with the grant's $803,909 limit.
# Scoring Criteria
|  |  |
| :- | :- |
| Criteria | Points available |
| Cost-Effectiveness     \- $/ton GHG reduced | 0 - 30 |
| Qualitative Benefits      \- Proximity to sensitive populations     \- Proximity to buildings to reduce energy costs     \- Proximity to sidewalks to shade well-traveled areas     \- Proximity to areas suggested by the CSC | 0 - 25 |
| Co-benefits     \- Criteria air pollutant emission reductions     \- Water and energy savings | 0 - 25 |
| Species Selection     \- Types of species of trees, bushes, and/or shrubs     \- Environmental benefits of the species selected     \- Consideration of initial size selection for the planting area to ensure the best chance for survival and growth  | 0 - 10 |
| Project Readiness     \- Timeliness of project implementation     \- Leveraging existing efforts in the community     \- Availability of external funding | 0 - 10 |
